Special cycle on Shtukas and categorical trace
Zeyu Wang

Abstract
. In this article, we relate the fake special cycle classes z 𝕃 σ , r z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma},r} attached to a Hecke eigensheaf 𝕃 σ ∈ Shv Nilp ​ ( Bun G ) \mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}) introduced in [ LW25 ] to the isotypic part of special cycles on Shtukas. As an application, we relate the self-intersection number of the isotypic part of special cycles arising from Rankin–Selberg period to higher derivatives of Rankin–Selberg L L -functions.



1.
Introduction

In
[
YZ17
]
[
YZ19
]
, a higher Gross–Zagier formula is proved, which for certain types of cuspidal automorphic representations
\pi
of
\PGL_{2}
over the function field
K(C)
for some curve
C
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, it relates the self-intersection numbers of the
\pi
-isotypic part of the Heegner–Drinfeld cycles on the moduli of
\PGL_{2}
-Shtukas with
r
-legs to the
r
-th derivative of the
L
-functions
L(\pi,s)
at the central point
s=1/2
. This formula can be regarded as a function field analog of the classical Gross–Zagier formula
[
GZ86
]
over the number field.

In
[
LW25
]
, the authors proved a formula relating a certain norm of some “fake” special cycle classes arising from Rankin–Selberg period to higher derivatives of Rankin–Selberg
L
-functions. More precisely, for geometrically irreducible Weil local system
\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n-1}
on
C
of rank
n,n-1
. Consider
M=H^{1}(C_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1})\oplus H^{1}(C_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},\sigma_{n}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*})
, which is a vector space naturally equipped with a symmetric bilinear form
\omega_{M}
arising from cup product. They defined some elements
z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}},r}\in(M^{\otimes r})^{*}
arising from taking Hecke composed with Frobenius trace of some Hecke operators on a geometric period integral, and proved a formula relating
\omega_{(M^{\otimes r})^{*}}(z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}},r},z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}},r})
with the
r
-th derivative of the Rankin–Selberg
L
-function
L(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}\oplus\sigma_{n}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s)
at the central value
s=1/2
. See
[
LW25
, Theorem 1.1]
for the precise formulation.

The formula proved in
[
LW25
]
was not about the intersection number of special cycles. Therefore, it cannot be regarded as a direct higher-dimensional analog of the higher Gross–Zagier formulas. Also, the fake special cycles considered in
loc.cit
are not seemingly natural from their definition. However, it was claimed in
loc.cit
that these seemingly artificially defined objects are indeed related to special cycle classes on Shtukas. The main subject of this article is to establish this relation. As an application, we prove a formula (Theorem
1.7
) relating self-intersection numbers of
\sigma=\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}
-isotypic part of special cycles on
\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
-Shtukas with higher derivatives of the Rankin–Selberg
L
-function
L(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}\oplus\sigma_{n}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s)
. This formula can be regarded as a direct generalization of the higher Gross–Zagier formula.

Two features in our formula are different from the higher Gross–Zagier formula in
[
YZ17
]
: The first difference is in the spectral decomposition of the cohomology of Shtukas. In
[
YZ17
]
, the spectral decomposition was made using the action of Hecke operators on the cohomology of Shtukas, in which the
\sigma
-isotypic part (actually,
\pi
-isotypic part for the automorphic representation
\pi
attached to
\sigma
) was the isotypic part for the associated Hecke character of
\pi
. In this article, we directly construct a subspace of the cohomology of Shtukas (rather than a complete spectral decomposition) and use it as our subspace of the
\sigma
-isotypic part. However, the geometric Langlands conjecture for
\GL_{n}
should imply that our
\sigma
-isotypic part is indeed the
\sigma
-isotypic part of the cohomology of Shtukas which can be defined using the (categorical) spectral action constructed in
[
AGK+22c
]
and the categorical trace interpretation of cohomology of Shtukas proved in
[
AGK+22a
]
. Therefore, our definition of
\sigma
-isotypic part should be completely canonical. The second difference lies in the definition of the isotypic part of special cycle classes and the intersection number. One significant difficulty in generalizing the work of
[
YZ17
]
to higher-dimensional cases is that the special cycles are rarely compact in general, which makes defining the intersection number a challenge. In
[
YZ17
]
, the special cycles are compact, and one can directly take their self-intersection number. However, the special cycles are not compact in our case, and we have to define the intersection number in an ad-hoc way. What we do is the following: Although the special cycles are not compact, one can still talk about their intersection numbers with compactly supported cohomology classes. Therefore, we regard these special cycles as functionals on the
\sigma
-isotypic part of the (compact support) cohomology of Shtukas (which are finite-dimensional vector spaces), on which the intersection pairing is non-degenerate. Therefore, we can define the “self-intersection number” as the quadratic norm of these functionals under the dual of the intersection pairings. As far as we know, the non-degeneracy of intersection pairings on the isotypic part, which plays an essential role in our definition, was not previously known.

1.1.
Main result: higher Rankin–Selberg integrals

In this section, we formulate our main result on the intersection number of Rankin–Selberg cycles on Shtukas. Our main result is Theorem
1.7
, which confirms a version of Conjecture
1.6
.

From §
1.1.1
to §
1.1.5
, we introduce notations and backgrounds. In §
1.1.6
and §
1.1.7
, we formulate the main result.

Throughout the article, we fix a smooth projective geometrically connected curve
C
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

1.1.1.
Moduli space of Shtukas

We first recall the definition of the moduli space of
\GL_{n}
-Shtukas.

Fix a positive integer
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Consider
I=\{1,2,\cdots,r\}
. Take
\{\pm 1\}^{r}_{0}\subset\{\pm 1\}^{r}
to be the subset consisting of sequences
\underline{\epsilon}=(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{r})
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\epsilon_{i}=0
. For each
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{r}
, we denote the moduli space of (iterated)
\GL_{n}
-Shtukas with
r
-legs and modification type
\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}
by
\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}
. It is is the moduli stack such that for any
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-scheme
S
, we have
\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}(S)
is the groupoid of tuples

((c_{i})_{i\in I},\mathcal{E}_{0}{\mathrel{\to}^{\epsilon_{1}\cdot c_{1}}}\mathcal{E}_{1}{\mathrel{\to}^{\epsilon_{2}\cdot c_{2}}}\cdots{\mathrel{\to}^{\epsilon_{r}\cdot c_{r}}}\mathcal{E}_{r},\alpha:\mathcal{E}_{0}\cong(\mathrm{id}_{C}\times\Frob_{S})^{*}\mathcal{E}_{r})

where:

•

c_{i}\in C(S)
for
i=1,\cdots,r
;

•

\mathcal{E}_{i}
is a vector bundle of rank
n
on
C\times S
for
i=0,\cdots,r
;

•

\mathcal{E}_{i-1}{\mathrel{\to}^{\epsilon_{i}\cdot c_{i}}}\mathcal{E}_{i}
is an isomorphism of vector bundles over
C\times S\backslash\Gamma_{c_{i}}
(
\Gamma_{c_{i}}\subset C\times S
is the graph of the map
c_{i}:S\to C
) such that

–

If
\epsilon_{i}=1
, the inverse of the map induces an inclusion
\mathcal{E}_{i}\subset\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{i-1}/\mathcal{E}_{i}
is supported on
\Gamma_{c_{i}}
on which it is locally-free of rank
1
;

–

If
\epsilon_{i}=-1
, the map induces an inclusion
\mathcal{E}_{i-1}\subset\mathcal{E}_{i}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{i}/\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
is supported on
\Gamma_{c_{i}}
on which it is locally-free of rank
1
;

•

\alpha:\mathcal{E}_{0}\cong(\mathrm{id}_{C}\times\Frob_{S})^{*}\mathcal{E}_{r}
is an isomorphism of vector bundles. Here
\Frob_{S}:S\to S
is the relative Frobenius map over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

The moduli space
\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}
turns out to be a Deligne-Mumford stack locally of finite type over
C^{I}
. We use
l_{I}:\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}\to C^{I}
to denote the natural map sending above data to
(c_{i})_{i\in I}
. The moduli space
\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}
is non-empty if and only if
\underline{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}^{r}_{0}
. We have
\dim\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}=nr
.

When
\underline{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}^{r}_{0}
, one has
\pi_{0}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}})\cong\mathbb{Z}
where the isomorphism is given by taking the degree of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
as a vector bundle over
C
. This gives a decomposition into connected components

(1.1)

\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}=\coprod_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{d}.

In our case, we are interested in the moduli space of
\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
-Shtukas

(1.2)

\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}=\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}\times_{C^{I}}\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}},

whose decomposition into connected components is

(1.3)

\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}=\coprod_{(d_{n},d_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(d_{n},d_{n-1})}

in which

\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(d_{n},d_{n-1})}=\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{d_{n}}\times_{C^{I}}\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{d_{n-1}}.

1.1.2.
Rankin–Selberg cycles

The classical story of (everywhere unramified) Rankin--Selberg integrals (over function fields) concerns the integration
1
1

1

Since we are over function fields, integration here means summation.
of
f\in\Fun_{c}(\Bun_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
(the vector space of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-valued functions with compact support) over the diagonal map
\pi(\mathbb{F}_{q}):\Bun_{\GL_{n-1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\Bun_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
in which the map
\Bun_{\GL_{n-1}}\to\Bun_{\GL_{n}}
is given by taking direct sum with the trivial line bundle. Or equivalently speaking, one is interested in the function
\pi(\mathbb{F}_{q})_{!}1_{\Bun_{\GL_{n-1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\in\Fun(\Bun_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
where
\pi(\mathbb{F}_{q})_{!}
is summation along fibers.

Note that
\Bun_{\GL_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}
for
r=0
. The principle of higher integrals is replacing the groupoid
\Bun_{G}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
by the moduli stack
\Sht_{G,I}
. In particular, in the Rankin–Selberg case, instead of considering the characteristic function
\pi(\mathbb{F}_{q})_{!}1_{\Bun_{\GL_{n-1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\in\Fun(\Bun_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
, one studies the
Rankin–Selberg cycle classes
(or cohomological Rankin–Selberg cycles)

(1.4)

\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}]\in H^{\BM}_{2(n-1)r}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}})

where

•

[\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}]\in H^{BM}_{2(n-1)r}(\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}})
is the fundamental class of
\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}
as a Borel-Moore homology class;

•

\pi_{\Sht,I}:\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}\to\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}
is the diagonal map which is taking direct sum with the trivial vector bundle on the first factor. The map
\pi_{\Sht,I}
is finite schematic by
[
Yun22
]
.

•

\pi_{\Sht,I,!}:H^{BM}_{2(n-1)r}(\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}})\to H^{\BM}_{2(n-1)r}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}})
is the push-forward of Borel-Moore homology class along proper maps.

Here, we consider Borel-Moore homology with
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-coefficient for stacks over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. For stacks defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, if not otherwise specified, we always consider the Borel-Moore homology (or cohomology) of its base change to
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. In this introduction, we drop all the Tate twist.

1.1.3.
Spectral decomposition of cohomology of Shtukas

In this article, by cohomology, we by default mean cohomology with compact support in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-coefficient of stacks over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. The cohomology of Shtukas admits a direct sum decomposition into isotypic parts for Langlands parameters.

To apply the machinery of geometric Langlands in positive characteristic, we need to pose the following assumption on the characteristic of the base field:

Assumption 1.1

(Assumption on characteristic)
.

Throughout the article, we keep the same assumption on the characteristic of the base field as in
[
GR25
, §0.1.9]
.

For each split reductive group
G
, we use
\check{G}
to denote the Langlands dual group. In
[
AGK+22c
, §24.1]
, the authors define a quasi-compact algebraic stack
\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{arith}}
over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
(denoted
\mathrm{LocSys}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{arithm}}(X)
for
X=C
in
loc.cit
) which is the moduli space of
\check{G}
-(Weil) local systems over
C
. This moduli stack has also been considered in
[
Zhu21
]
.

The ring of global sections
\Gamma(\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{arith}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{arith}}})
is called the
algebra of excursion operators
. An equivalent form of it was first considered by
[
GL18
]
. This algebra naturally acts on the cohomology of
G
-Shtukas. In this formulation, the existence of this action is a consequence of
[
AGK+22a
, Main Theorem 0.3.10]
.

In our case, we get an action of
\Gamma(\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}})
on
\Gamma_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})
for each
\underline{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}^{r}
. It gives rise to a direct sum decomposition

(1.5)

\Gamma_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})=\bigoplus_{s\in\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}})}\Gamma_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})_{s}

where
\Gamma_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})_{s}
is supported on the connected component of
\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}
indexed by
s
.

For a Weil local system
\sigma_{n}\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
, we use
\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G},\sigma_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}\subset\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{arith}}
to denote its underlying connected component. We use
\Gamma_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})_{\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G},\sigma_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}}
to denote the corresponding term in the spectral decomposition (
1.5
).

The action above extends to an action of
H^{*}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}})
on
\prod_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{*}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{d},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})
. We use

(1.6)

(\prod_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{*}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{d},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma_{n}}\subset\prod_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{*}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{d},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})

to denote the maximal sub-module (scheme theoretically) supported on
\sigma_{n}\in\Spec H^{0}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}})
. For an irreducible Weil local system
\sigma_{n}
, this sub-module turns out to be a perfect complex, and it is what we mean by the
\sigma_{n}
-isotypic part of the cohomology of Shtukas. Note that we are taking a direct product instead of a direct sum here to avoid getting an empty sub-module.

We use
\overline{\sigma}_{n}
to denote the base change of
\sigma_{n}
to
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, which we call the underlying geometric local system of
\sigma_{n}
. We say that
\sigma_{n}
is geometrically irreducible if
\overline{\sigma}_{n}
is irreducible. If
\sigma_{n}
is geometrically irreducible, the underlying connected component
\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n},\sigma_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}
is isomorphic to
[\mathbb{G}_{m}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
in which
\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts trivially. Here, the first
\mathbb{G}_{m}
can be regarded as the moduli of different Weil sheaf structures on the underlying geometric local system
\overline{\sigma}_{n}
. The second
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is the moduli of automorphisms of
\sigma_{n}
. In this case, the isotypic part defined above admits the following conjectural description:

Conjecture 1.2
.

For each
\underline{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}^{r}_{0}
, there is a canonical isomorphism

\Gamma_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})_{\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n},\sigma_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}}[(n-1)r]\cong\Gamma(C^{I},\sigma_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n},\sigma_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}})

where
\sigma_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}=\boxtimes_{i\in I}\sigma_{n}^{\epsilon_{i}}
. In particular, it gives a canonical isomorphism

(1.7)

(\prod_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{*+(n-1)r}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{d},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma_{n}}\cong H^{*}(C^{I},\sigma_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}).

Remark 1.3
.

Conjecture
1.2
is a consequence of the geometric Langlands conjecture with restricted variation for
\GL_{n}
in characteristic
p
formulated in
[
AGK+22c
]
. In particular, it is a consequence of
[
GR25
]
.

Remark 1.4
.

While the isomorphism in Conjecture
1.2
is claimed to be canonical, there are several different canonical choices indeed. Choice of such an isomorphism can be roughly thought of as a Shtuka analog of the choice of a Hecke eigenform: one can choose it to be Whittaker normalized,
L^{2}
-normalized… Whenever using such an isomorphism, one should specify which isomorphism is being used.

For
\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
-Shtukas, one can similarly define the action by excursion operators

H^{*}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{arith}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{arith}}})=H^{*}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}})\otimes H^{*}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{arith}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{arith}}})

on

\prod_{(d_{n},d_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}H^{*}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(d_{n},d_{n-1})},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}).

One can define the
\sigma=(\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n-1})\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{arith}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
-isotypic part

(1.8)

(\prod_{(d_{n},d_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}H^{*}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(d_{n},d_{n-1})},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma}\subset\prod_{(d_{n},d_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}H^{*}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}).

1.1.4.
Intersection pairing

The cohomology of
G
-Shtukas carries an intersection pairing. When
G=\GL_{n}
, we have the intersection pairing

(1.9)

\langle-,-\rangle_{\underline{\epsilon}}^{d}:H_{c}^{nr}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{d},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})\otimes H_{c}^{nr}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{d},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})\xrightarrow{\cup}H_{c}^{2nr}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{d},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})\cong\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}

induced by cup product and taking the degree of
0
-cycles. The following concerns the behavior of the intersection pairing on the
\sigma_{n}
-isotypic part (
1.6
):

Conjecture 1.5
.

For each
d\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\underline{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}^{r}_{0}
, and irreducible Weil local system
\sigma_{n}\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{arith}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
, the restriction of the intersection pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{\underline{\epsilon}}^{d}
defines a non-degenerate bilinear form

(1.10)

\langle-,-\rangle_{\underline{\epsilon},\sigma_{n}}^{d}:(\prod_{e\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{c}^{nr}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{e},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma_{n}}\otimes(\prod_{e\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{c}^{nr}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{e},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma_{n}^{*}}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}.

Given Conjecture
1.5
, we can use the bilinear form
\langle-,-\rangle_{\underline{\epsilon},\sigma_{n}}^{d}
to make an identification

(\prod_{e\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{c}^{nr}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{e},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma_{n}^{*}}^{*}\cong(\prod_{e\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{c}^{nr}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{e},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma_{n}}.

This gives a non-degenerate bilinear form

(1.11)

\langle-,-\rangle_{\underline{\epsilon},\sigma_{n}}^{d,*}:(\prod_{e\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{c}^{nr}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{e},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma_{n}}^{*}\otimes(\prod_{e\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{c}^{nr}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}^{e},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma_{n}^{*}}^{*}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}.

For
\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
-Shtukas and an irreducible Weil local system
\sigma=(\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n-1})\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{arith}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
, consider
\sigma^{*}=(\sigma_{n}^{*},\sigma_{n-1}^{*})
. One can similarly define a bilinear form

(1.12)

\begin{split}\langle-,-\rangle_{\underline{\epsilon},\sigma}^{(d_{n},d_{n-1}),*}:(\prod_{(e_{n},e_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}H^{2(n-1)r}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(e_{n},e_{n-1})},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma}^{*}\otimes\\
(\prod_{(e_{n},e_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}H^{2(n-1)r}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(e_{n},e_{n-1})},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma^{*}}^{*}\to\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}\end{split}.

In this setting, we also expect Conjecture
1.5
.

1.1.5.
L
-function of local system

For a Weil local system
\sigma
on
C
, its
L
-function is defined by

(1.13)

L(\sigma,s)=\det(1-q^{-s}\Frob,\Gamma(C,\sigma))^{-1}=\prod_{i=0}^{2}\det(1-q^{-s}\Frob,H^{i}(C,\sigma))^{(-1)^{i-1}}.

The root number is defined by

(1.14)

\epsilon(\sigma)=\det(\Frob,\Gamma(C,\sigma)(\frac{1}{2}))^{-1}=\prod_{i=0}^{2}\det(q^{-1/2}\Frob,H^{i}(C,\sigma))^{(-1)^{i-1}}.

1.1.6.
Statement of the main conjecture

Before stating our main result, we will first formulate a conjecture, which will be confirmed by our main result under some assumptions that are satisfied in the most interesting cases.

Borel-Moore homology can be naturally regarded as the dual of cohomology with compact support. In our case, we have an isomorphism

H^{\BM}_{2(n-1)r}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}})\cong H_{c}^{2(n-1)r}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})^{*}.

Therefore, one can view the special cycle classes (
1.4
) as functionals on the cohomology with compact support and study its restriction on the
\sigma
-isotypic part (
1.8
). This gives

(1.15)

(\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{d}])_{\sigma}\in(\prod_{(e_{n},e_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}H^{2(n-1)r}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(e_{n},e_{n-1})},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma}^{*}.

We have the following conjecture:

Conjecture 1.6
.

Assume
\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n-1}
are irreducible Weil local systems on
C
with rank
n,n-1
. Take
\sigma=(\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n-1})\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{arith}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
. Then
(\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{h}])_{\sigma}
is non-zero for finitely many
h\in\mathbb{Z}
. Take

(1.16)

\begin{split}(\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}])_{\sigma}=\sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}}(\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{h}])_{\sigma}\\
\in(\prod_{(e_{n},e_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}H^{2(n-1)r}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(e_{n},e_{n-1})},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma}^{*}.\end{split}

For any
(d_{n},d_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we have

(1.17)

\begin{split}\sum_{\underline{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}^{r}_{0}}\langle(\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}])_{\sigma},(\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}])_{\sigma^{*}}\rangle_{\underline{\epsilon},\sigma}^{(d_{n},d_{n-1}),*}\\
=q^{\dim\Bun_{\GL_{n-1}}}(\ln q)^{-r-2}\frac{\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{r}\Big|_{s=1/2}\widetilde{L}(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}\oplus\sigma_{n}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s)}{\Res_{s=1}\widetilde{L}(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n}^{*},s)\Res_{s=1}\widetilde{L}(\sigma_{n-1}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s)}\end{split}.

Here
\dim\Bun_{\GL_{n-1}}=(n-1)^{2}(g-1)
where
g
is the genus of the curve
C
. The normalized
L
-functions are defined as

(1.18)

\widetilde{L}(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}\oplus\sigma_{n}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s)=q^{2n(n-1)(g-1)(s-1/2)}L(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}\oplus\sigma_{n}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s)

(1.19)

\widetilde{L}(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n}^{*},s)=q^{n^{2}(g-1)s}L(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n}^{*},s)

(1.20)

\widetilde{L}(\sigma_{n-1}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s)=q^{(n-1)^{2}(g-1)s}L(\sigma_{n-1}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s).

1.1.7.
Statement of the main result

Our main result confirms a slightly different formulation of Conjecture
1.6
when
\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n-1}
are geometrically irreducible.

In this case, instead of working with the subspace

(1.21)

\begin{split}(\prod_{(e_{n},e_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}H^{2(n-1)r}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(e_{n},e_{n-1})},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}))_{\sigma}\\
\subset\\
\prod_{(e_{n},e_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}H^{2(n-1)r}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(e_{n},e_{n-1})},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})\end{split}

defined via the spectral action, we directly construct an injection

(1.22)

H^{*}\xi_{\sigma,\underline{\epsilon}}:H^{r}(C^{I},(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}})\to\prod_{(e_{n},e_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}H^{2(n-1)r}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{(e_{n},e_{n-1})},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}).

The image of
H^{*}\xi_{\sigma,\underline{\epsilon}}
should coincide with the subspace (
1.21
): While we will not show this in this article, we believe it is an easy consequence of the geometric Langlands conjecture for
\GL_{n}
, in particular, the result of
[
GR25
]
. Therefore, the map
H^{*}\xi_{\sigma,\underline{\epsilon}}
should give a choice of the isomorphism (
1.7
). The construction of this map will be given in §
6.1
.

Throughout the article after this point, we will always use (
1.22
) as our
\sigma
-isotypic part. The meaning of (
1.15
) and (
1.12
) will also be adapted to the isotypic part (
1.22
). We can formulate our main theorem, which will be proved in §
6.4
:

Theorem 1.7
.

After replacing the subspace (
1.21
) by (
1.22
) and assuming

•

\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n-1}
are geometrically irreducible,

•

p>n
,

the Conjecture
1.5
and Conjecture
1.6
hold.

Remark 1.8
.

The first assumption is used for simplicity in constructing a Hecke eigensheaf. The second assumption comes from Assumption
1.1
.

1.2.
Idea of proof of the main result

In this section, we explain the idea of proof of Theorem
1.7
.

From now on, we take
k=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
. We work with a split reductive group
G
. To prove Theorem
1.7
, we compute explicitly all the terms on the left-hand side of (
1.17
) so that we can explicitly compare both sides. This includes writing down explicitly the isotypic part of Rankin–Selberg cycle classes
(\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}^{h}])_{\sigma}
as well as the isotypic part of the intersection pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{\underline{\epsilon},\sigma}^{(d_{n},d_{n-1}),*}
. These two elements can be uniformly understood by taking categorical trace of the corresponding geometric relative Langlands statements in
[
BZSV24
]
.

1.2.1.
Categorical trace

We first briefly explain the formalism of categorical trace. We refer to §
2.1
for a more precise explanation. For a dualizable presentable
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathcal{C}
with an endomorphism
F\in\End(\mathcal{C})
, its categorical trace is a space
\operatorname{tr}(F,\mathcal{C})
. Therefore, to understand a space
S
, one can try to find a pair
(\mathcal{C},F)
such that
S\cong\operatorname{tr}(F,\mathcal{C})
. This allows one to study the richer category
\mathcal{C}
instead of the space
S
.

In our case, we would like to understand a morphism between spaces
z:S_{1}\to S_{2}
. This can be achieved by the formalism of the functoriality of the categorical trace. Suppose we can write
S_{1}\cong\operatorname{tr}(F_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
and
S_{2}\cong\operatorname{tr}(F_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2})
. For each continuous (i.e. colimit preserving) functor
L:\mathcal{C}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}_{2}
admitting a continuous right adjoint, given a natural transformation
\eta:L\circ F_{1}\to F_{2}\circ L
, there is an induced morphism between spaces
\operatorname{tr}(\eta):\operatorname{tr}(F_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})\to\operatorname{tr}(F_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2})
. Therefore, one could look for a natural transformation
\eta
such that
\operatorname{tr}(\eta)=z
, which changes the study of a map between spaces to the study of a natural transformation.

The discussion above also makes sense when working with categories linear over a symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{A}
. In that case, one replaces the word “spaces” by “objects in
\mathcal{A}
” and “functors” by “
\mathcal{A}
-linear functors”. We use
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{C})\in\mathcal{A}
to denote the (
\mathcal{A}
-linear) categorical trace. See Example
2.4
for a more precise treatment.

1.2.2.
Fundamental diagram

In this section, we pretend that
G
is semisimple, which is unfortunately not the case since we care about the case
G=\GL_{n}
. However, this would simplify the situation and is enough for explaining the idea.

In our setting, we would like to understand the sequence of morphisms

(1.23)

V_{\sigma}^{I}\langle-d_{I}\rangle\xrightarrow{\xi_{\sigma,I}}l_{I,!}(\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}}\langle-d_{I}\rangle)\xrightarrow{[Z_{V^{I}}^{X}]}\underline{k}_{C^{I}}.

Here,

•

I
is a finite set,
V^{I}\in\Rep(\check{G}^{I})
,
\sigma\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{arith}}(k)
is a Langlands parameter,
V_{\sigma}^{I}
is the local system on
C^{I}
involved in the Tannakian definition of
\sigma
,
\langle n\rangle=\Pi(\frac{n}{2})[n]
is the shearing. Here
\Pi
means changing the parity, which can be safely ignored at this point.
d_{I}\in\mathbb{Z}
;

•

\Sht_{G,I}
is the moduli of
G
-Shtukas with
I
-legs without bounding the poles.
\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Sht_{G,I})
is the sheaf attached by the geometric Satake equivalence.
l_{I}:\Sht_{G,I}\to C^{I}
is the map remembering only the legs. When
G=\GL_{n}
,
V^{I}=\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}
,
\sigma=\sigma_{n}
,
d_{I}=r
, we have
\Gamma_{c}(l_{I,!}(\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}}\langle-d_{I}\rangle))=\Gamma_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})\langle(n-2)r\rangle
.

•

\underline{k}_{C^{I}}
is the constant sheaf on
C^{I}
;

•

\xi_{\sigma,I}:V_{\sigma}^{I}\to l_{I,!}(\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}})
is the
\sigma
-isotypic part map. In
\GL_{n}
-case as above, ignoring the difference between direct sum and direct product, we have
H^{*}\xi_{\sigma,I}=H^{*}\xi_{\sigma_{n},\underline{\epsilon}}:H^{*}(C^{I},\sigma_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}})\to H^{*+(n-1)r}_{c}(\Sht_{\GL_{n},\underline{\epsilon}},\underline{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})((n-1)r/2)
which is the
\GL_{n}
-version of the map (
1.22
).

•

[Z_{V^{I}}^{X}]
is a special cycle class attached to an affine smooth
G
-variety
X
. In this article, we are particularly interested in two cases:

–

Rankin–Selberg case:
G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
,
X=\GL_{n-1}\backslash\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
,
V^{I}=(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n-1})^{\underline{\epsilon}}
, and

[Z_{V^{I}}^{X}]=\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{\GL_{n-1},\Std_{n-1}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}].

–

Group case:
G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
,
X=\GL_{n}\backslash\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
,
V^{I}=(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n})^{\underline{\epsilon}}
, and

[Z_{V^{I}}^{X}]=\Delta_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}]=\langle-,-\rangle_{\underline{\epsilon}}.

Here,
\Delta_{\Sht,I}:\Sht_{\GL_{n},\Std_{n}^{\underline{\epsilon}}}\to\Sht_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n},(\Std_{n}\boxtimes\Std_{n})^{\underline{\epsilon}}}
is the diagonal map.

Following the principle in §
1.2.1
, we switch to understanding a sequence of functors between categories with endomorphisms. We consider the following
fundamental diagram
:

(1.24)

This diagram contains a wealth of information. Let us explain it step by step.
We first explain the categories in (
1.24
):

•

\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})
is the category of sheaves with lissé cohomologies on
C^{I}
defined in
[
AGK+22c
, Definition 1.2.6]
;

•

\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
is the category of sheaves on
\Bun_{G}
with nilpotent singular support. This is the main player in the geometric Langlands with restricted variation
[
AGK+22c
]
.

We then explain the functors between categories, which are horizontal maps in (
1.24
):

•

\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
is a choice of Hecke eigensheaf with eigenvalue
\sigma\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}(k)
. When
G=\GL_{n}
and
\sigma=\sigma_{n}
is geometrically irreducible, such an eigensheaf is constructed in
[
FGV02
]
.

•

\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp},I}=\Gamma_{c}(-\otimes\mathcal{P}_{X})\otimes\mathrm{id}
where
\mathcal{P}_{X}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})
is the period sheaf attached to the affine smooth
G
-variety
X
as before. See
[
BZSV24
, §10.3]
. We are particularly interested in two cases:

–

G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
,
X=\GL_{n-1}\backslash\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{X}=\pi_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{\GL_{n-1}}}
where

\pi:\Bun_{\GL_{n-1}}\to\Bun_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}}.

–

G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
,
X=\GL_{n}\backslash\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}_{X}=\Delta_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{\GL_{n}}}
where

\Delta:\Bun_{\GL_{n}}\to\Bun_{\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}}.

Now we come to the endomorphisms of categories which are vertical maps in (
1.24
):

•

T_{V^{I}}:\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\otimes\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})\to\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\otimes\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})
is the Hecke operator attached to
V^{I}\in\Rep(\check{G}^{I})
.

•

\Frob:\Bun_{G}\to\Bun_{G}
is the (relative) Frobenius defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

Finally, we explain the natural transformations in (
1.24
):

•

The natural transformation
\eta_{\sigma}^{(1)}
comes from the Frobenius equivariant structure and Hecke eigen-property of
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
.

•

The natural transformation
\eta_{\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X},\mathrm{Nilp}}^{(1)}
comes from a cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X}
on the period sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{X}
. The procedure of attaching such a natural transformation to a cohomological correspondence will be explained in §
2.2.6
.

Pretending that the horizontal maps preserve compact objects, by the functoriality of categorical trace applied to (
1.24
), we get the upper row in the diagram:

(1.25)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}(-\otimes V_{\sigma}^{I}\langle-d_{I}\rangle,\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}((\Frob\times\mathrm{id})_{!}\circ T_{V^{I}\langle-d_{I}\rangle},\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\otimes\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}(\mathrm{id},\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\sigma}^{I}\langle-d_{I}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{I,!}(\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}}\langle-d_{I}\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{k}_{C^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}(\eta_{\sigma}^{(1)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}(\eta_{\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X},\mathrm{Nilp}}^{(1)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{LT}^{\mathrm{Serre}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{\sigma,I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[Z_{V^{I}}^{X}]}

.

Note that the lower row of the diagram (
1.25
) is (
1.23
).

We first explain the vertical isomorphisms in (
1.25
):

•

The left and right vertical isomorphisms follow from the most obvious computation of categorical trace.

•

The middle map
\mathrm{LT}^{\mathrm{Serre}}
is defined in
[
AGK+22a
, §5.4]
. The fact that it is an isomorphism is the main subject of
[
AGK+22a
]
. See §
4.2
for a discussion.

We now explain the commutativity of the diagram (
1.25
):

•

The commutativity of the left square defines the
\sigma
-isotypic part map
\xi_{\sigma,I}:V_{\sigma}^{I}\to l_{I,!}(\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}})
.

•

The commutativity of the right square will be illustrated in §
1.3
. In this article, we prove it under Assumption
2.36
.

Remark 1.9
.

In the discussion above, we pretend that the horizontal functors in (
1.24
) preserve compact objects. While this is usually satisfied when
G
is semisimple and
\sigma
is irreducible, it mostly fails for non-semisimple split reductive groups, which unfortunately includes the case
G=\GL_{n}
. In §
5.3
, we will discuss a replacement of (
1.24
) for general split reductive groups.

1.2.3.
Fake special cycle classes

Now we only need to understand the outer square of (
1.25
). For this, we need to understand the natural transformation
\eta_{\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X},\mathrm{Nilp}}^{(1)}\circ\eta_{\sigma}^{(1)}
for the outer square of (
1.24
), and we need to understand how to compute
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}(\eta_{\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X},\mathrm{Nilp}}^{(1)}\circ\eta_{\sigma}^{(1)})
. This is the study of fake special cycle classes, which is the main subject of
[
LW25
]
.

From the cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X}
, one obtains a map

(1.26)

a_{\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X},\sigma}:V_{\sigma}^{I}\langle-d_{I}\rangle\otimes\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\to\underline{k}_{C^{I}}\otimes\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}.

Under the assumption that horizontal maps in (
1.24
) preserve compact objects, the complex
\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
is a perfect complex. Therefore, one arrives at the
fake special cycle class

(1.27)

\begin{split}z_{\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X},\sigma}:V_{\sigma}^{I}\langle-d_{I}\rangle&\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}}}V_{\sigma}^{I}\langle-d_{I}\rangle\otimes\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\otimes(\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})^{*}\\
&\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\Frob\otimes\mathrm{id}}V_{\sigma}^{I}\langle-d_{I}\rangle\otimes\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\otimes(\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})^{*}\\
&\xrightarrow{a_{\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X},\sigma}\otimes\mathrm{id}}\underline{k}_{C^{I}}\otimes\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\otimes(\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})^{*}\\
&\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\ev_{\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}}}\underline{k}_{C^{I}}\end{split}.

See (
5.3
) for an equivalent definition of
z_{\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X},\sigma}
. These elements have been studied in
[
LW25
, §1.1.5]
. It turns out that the composition of the top horizontal map in (
1.25
) coincides with
z_{\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X},\sigma}
.

We are particularly interested in the following cases:

•

When
G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
,
X=\GL_{n-1}\backslash\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
. The fake special cycle classes (
1.27
) are understood via Theorem
6.5
.

•

When
G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
,
X=\GL_{n}\backslash\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
. The fake special cycle classes are described in Theorem
6.12
.

During the proof of results above, we heavily use the automorphic commutator relation developed in
[
LW25
, §4]
. See §
5.1.3
for a brief recollection.

1.3.
Main tool: Categorical trace interpretation of special cycle classes

In this section, we explain in more detail the right commutative square of (
1.25
). It turns out we can make sense of it and prove it in two cases:

•

Homogeneous minuscule case:
G
is split reductive,
X=H\backslash G
for some spherical split reductive subgroup
H\subset G
,
V^{I}\in\Rep(\check{G}^{I})
is irreducible minuscule,
[Z_{V^{I}}^{X}]=\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{H,V_{H}^{I}}]
for some irreducible minuscule representation
V_{H}^{I}\in\Rep(\check{H}^{I})
. This includes all the cases we need in the proof of Theorem
1.7
and will be introduced in §
1.3.1
.

•

Diagonal case:
H
is split reductive,
G=H\times H
,
X=H\backslash H\times H
,
V^{I}=V_{H}^{I}\boxtimes V_{H}^{I}\in\Rep(\check{G}^{I})
for some irreducible representation
V_{H}^{I}\in\Rep(\check{H}^{I})
. This case will be introduced in §
1.3.2
. This case in general will not be used in the proof of Theorem
1.7
, but we will use it to prove the non-degeneracy of intersection pairing for
\GL_{n}
with arbitrary coweight in Corollary
6.13
, which has its own interest.

1.3.1.
Minuscule homogeneous special cycle classes

Consider a split connected reductive group
G
and a spherical split connected reductive subgroup
H
. Take
X=H\backslash G
. We choose maximal tori
T\subset G
and
T_{H}\subset H
such that
T_{H}\subset T
. We use
X_{*}(T)
,
X_{*}(T_{H})
to denote the coweight lattices.

For the finite set
I=\{1,2\cdots,r\}
and a sequence of minuscule coweights
\lambda_{H,I}=(\lambda_{H,1},\cdots,\lambda_{H,r})\in X_{*}(T_{H})^{I}
, we obtain a sequence of coweights
\lambda_{I}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{r})\in X_{*}(T)^{I}
such that
\lambda_{i}
in the image of
\lambda_{H,i}
under the natural map
X_{*}(T_{H})\subset X_{*}(T)
. Let
V_{\lambda_{I}}\in\Rep(\check{G}^{I})
be the irreducible representation with highest weight
\lambda_{I}
. We use
\Sht_{G,\lambda_{I}}\subset\Sht_{G,I}
to denote the closed Schubert cell of type
\lambda_{I}
.

We define the
minuscule homogeneous special cycle class

[Z_{V^{I}}^{X}]=\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}]\in H_{-d_{\lambda_{I}}+2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{BM}(\Sht_{G,I}/C^{I},\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}})

in which

H_{-d_{\lambda_{I}}+2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{BM}(\Sht_{G,{\lambda_{I}}}/C^{I},\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}})=\Hom^{0}(l_{I,!}(\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}})\langle-d_{\lambda_{I}}+2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle,\underline{k}_{C^{I}}).

Here,

•

\pi_{\Sht,I}:\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}\to\Sht_{G,\lambda_{I}}
is finite and schematic by
[
Yun22
]
.
3
3

3

In some part of this article, we write
\Sht_{G,\lambda_{I}}=\Sht_{G,V_{\lambda_{I}}}
.

•

[\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}]\in H_{2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{BM}(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I})\cong H_{2(d_{\lambda_{H,I}}+r)}^{BM}(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}})
is the (relative) fundamental class.

•

l_{I}:\Sht_{G,\lambda_{I}}\to C^{I}
is the map remembering only the legs.

•

d_{\lambda_{H,I}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\langle 2\rho_{H},\lambda_{H,i}\rangle=\dim(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}})-\dim(C^{I})
,
d_{\lambda_{I}}=\sum_{i}\langle 2\rho_{G},\lambda_{i}\rangle
.

We now explain the right square in (
1.24
). We first explain the Hecke operator
T_{V^{I}}
. Consider the correspondence

\hbox to229.54pt{\vbox to22.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 114.76889pt\lower-9.2964pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-114.76889pt}{-2.77559pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

For
V^{I}\in\Rep(\check{G}^{I})^{\heartsuit}
, via the geometric Satake equivalence, one obtains a (super) sheaf
\IC_{V^{I}}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G,I})
normalized such that it is perverse on each fiber of
\Hk_{G,I}\to C^{I}
with parity same as
\sum_{i\in I}\langle 2\rho_{G},\lambda_{i}\rangle
. This gives the Hecke operator

T_{V^{I}}=\overrightarrow{h}_{I,*}(\overleftarrow{h}_{I}^{!}(-)\otimes^{!}\IC_{V^{I}})\cong\overrightarrow{h}_{I,!}(\overleftarrow{h}_{I}^{*}(-)\otimes\IC_{V^{I}}):\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})\to\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})

which preserves the full-subcategory
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\otimes\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})\subset\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})
.

We now turn to the definition of the natural transformation
\eta_{\mathfrak{c}_{V^{I}}^{X},\mathrm{Nilp}}^{(1)}=\eta^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}},\mathrm{Nilp}}
via cohomological correspondence. We leave it to §
2.2
for notations and operations involving cohomological correspondences.

We have the relative fundamental class of
\overleftarrow{h}_{H,I}:\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}\to\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}
which can be regarded as a cohomological correspondence

\begin{split}[\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}]&\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}},\underline{k}\langle 2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}},\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}})\\
&=H_{2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{BM}(\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I})\\
&=\Hom^{0}(\overrightarrow{h}_{H,I}^{*}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}}\langle 2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle,\overleftarrow{h}_{H,I}^{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}})\end{split}.

We apply the push-forward of cohomological correspondence in §
2.2.2
. Since that the right square of (
1.28
) is pushable, given
(\pi\times\mathrm{id})_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}}
, one can define the
minuscule homogeneous cohomological correspondence

\begin{split}\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}}:=\pi_{\Hk,I,!}[\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}]&\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}},\underline{k}\langle 2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle}(\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}},\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})\\
&=\Hom^{0}(\overrightarrow{h}_{I}^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})\langle 2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle),\overleftarrow{h}_{I}^{!}(\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})\\
&\cong\Hom^{0}(T_{c^{*}V_{\lambda_{I}}}\langle-d_{\lambda_{I}}+2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle(\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}}),\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})\end{split}

where
c^{*}
is the pull-back along the Cartan involution
c:\check{G}^{I}\to\check{G}^{I}
.

Composing with the natural isomorphism
\Frob^{*}\mathcal{P}_{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{P}_{X}
, one gets a cohomological correspondence

(1.29)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}}^{(1)}&\in\Hom^{0}(\overrightarrow{h}_{I}^{*}(\Frob\times\mathrm{id})^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})\langle 2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle,\overleftarrow{h}_{I}^{!}(\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}}))\\
&\cong\Hom^{0}(T_{c^{*}V_{\lambda_{I}}}\langle-d_{\lambda_{I}}+2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle\circ(\Frob\times\mathrm{id})^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}}),\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})\end{split}.

By projection formula, we have natural isomorphisms

l_{I,!}(T_{V^{I}}(-)\otimes-)\cong l_{I,!}(-\otimes T_{c^{*}V^{I}}(-))

and

l_{I,!}(\Frob^{*}(-)\otimes-)\cong l_{I,!}(-\otimes\Frob_{!}(-))

where
l_{I}:\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}\to C^{I}
. Since
\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp},I}=l_{I,!}(-\otimes(\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}}))
, the cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}}^{(1)}
gives us a natural transformation

\eta^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}},\mathrm{Nilp}}:\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp},I}\circ(\Frob\times\mathrm{id})_{!}\circ T_{V_{\lambda_{I}}}\langle-d_{\lambda_{I}}+2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle\to\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp},I}.

The main result in this part is the following:

Theorem 1.10
.

Assuming Assumption
2.36
, the right square of (
1.25
) is commutative for homogeneous minuscule special cycle classes. That is, we have a commutative square

(1.30)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}((\Frob\times\mathrm{id})_{!}\circ T_{V_{\lambda_{I}}}\langle-d_{\lambda_{I}}+2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle,\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\otimes\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}(\mathrm{id},\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{I,!}(\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}})\langle-d_{\lambda_{I}}+2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{k}_{C^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{LT}^{\mathrm{Serre}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}(\eta^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}},\mathrm{Nilp}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}]}

1.3.2.
Diagonal cycle classes

When
G=H\times H
and
H\subset G
is the diagonal embedding. Take
X=H\backslash H\times H
. In this section, we consider special cycle classes for possibly non-minuscule modification types. Note that the construction of the natural transformation
\eta_{\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}},\mathrm{Nilp}}^{(1)}
from the cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}}
makes sense without assuming that
\lambda_{H,I}
is minuscule. Only when defining
\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}}
and
[Z_{V^{I}}^{X}]
, the steps involving fundamental classes need a clarification.

The diagram (
1.28
) becomes

(1.31)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}_{H,I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}_{H,I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\Hk,I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}_{I}}

where
\Delta:\Bun_{H}\to\Bun_{G}
is the diagonal map.

In this case, we consider relative fundamental class

[\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}]\in H_{0}^{BM}(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I},\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{\otimes 2}|_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}})=\Hom^{0}(\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{\otimes 2}|_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}},\omega_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}})

given by the canonical map

\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{\otimes 2}|_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}}\cong\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}|_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}}\otimes\mathbb{D}_{C^{I}}(\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}|_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}})\to\omega_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}}.

Here we are using
\mathbb{D}_{C^{I}}:=\underline{\Hom}(-,\omega_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}}):\mathrm{Shv}(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}})\to\mathrm{Shv}(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}})
to denote the (relative) Verdier duality over
C^{I}
and
\omega_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}}
to denote the (relative) dualizing sheaf. A similar construction gives the relative fundamental class

[\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}]\in H_{0}^{BM}(\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I},\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{\otimes 2}).

We call
\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}}=\Delta_{\Hk,I,!}[\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}]
the
diagonal cohomological correspondence
.

In this case, the element

(1.32)

\langle-,-\rangle_{\lambda_{H,I}}:=\Delta_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}]:(l_{H,I,!}(\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}|_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}}))^{\otimes 2}\to\underline{k}_{C^{I}}

is called the
diagonal cycle class
and has a significant meaning: It is the intersection pairing on the compact support cohomology of Shtukas. The functor

\ev:=\int_{X,\mathrm{Nilp}}:\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})^{\otimes 2}\to\mathrm{Vect}

also has a significant meaning: It is the counit for the miraculous duality of
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
.

We have the following parallel result of Theorem
1.10
:

Theorem 1.11
.

The right square of (
1.25
) is commutative for diagonal special cycle classes. That is, we have a commutative square

(1.33)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}((\Frob\times\mathrm{id})_{!}\circ T_{V^{I}},\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\otimes\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}(\mathrm{id},\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(l_{H,I,!}(\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}|_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}}))^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{k}_{C^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{LT}^{\mathrm{Serre}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}(\eta^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}},\mathrm{Nilp}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle-,-\rangle_{\lambda_{H,I}}}

1.3.3.
Strategy of proof

The proof of Theorem
1.10
and Theorem
1.11
will be given in §
4.5
. One may think Theorem
1.10
and Theorem
1.11
are purely formal consequences of the six-functor formalism given the deep result of
[
AGK+22a
]
. While we do not regard them as deep results themselves, there are some seemingly necessary and non-trivial ingredients involved in the proof.

We only explain the proof of Theorem
1.10
since the proof of Theorem
1.11
is similar after the development of the theory of cohomological correspondences with kernels, which is the main subject of §
2.2
.

We prove Theorem
1.10
by establishing a two-step equality

\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}(\eta^{(1)}_{\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}},\mathrm{Nilp}})=\operatorname{tr}_{\Sht,C^{I}}(\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}})=\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}].

Here, the element
\operatorname{tr}_{\Sht,C^{I}}(\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}})\in H_{-d_{\lambda_{I}}+2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{BM}(\Sht_{G,{\lambda_{I}}}/C^{I},\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}})
is the geometric trace of the cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}^{(1)}_{\lambda_{H,I}}
. The notion of geometric trace of cohomological correspondence
4
4

4

In literature, it is also called sheaf-cycle correspondence.
has been widely used in literature. An incomplete list of its usage includes
[
FYZ23
]
[
YZ17
]
[
Var07
]
[
GV24
]
. It can be regarded as a generalization of the sheaf-function correspondence, in which case the correspondence is taken to be the Frobenius morphism.

The first equality follows from a direct diagram chasing and is completely formal given
[
AGK+22a
]
. Its proof will be provided in §
4
.

The second equality is more subtle: It involves compatibility between the push-forward of cohomological correspondence and the geometric trace construction. Its proof will be given in §
3
. Note that such compatibility is proved in
[
Var07
]
[
LZ22
]
for push-forward along proper maps. The problem here is that while the map
\pi_{\Sht,I}:\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}\to\Sht_{G,\lambda_{I}}
is proper, the map
\pi:\Bun_{H}\to\Bun_{G}
(or more essentially the map
\pi_{\Hk,I}:\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}\to\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}
) is not proper. We remedy this by showing that the map
\pi:\Bun_{H}\to\Bun_{G}
(hence the map
\pi_{\Hk,I}:\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}\to\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}
) admits a compactification such that the Hecke-Frobenius correspondence is
contracting along the boundary
. The idea of studying contracting correspondence in the theory of cohomological correspondence is not new: It appears in
[
Var07
]
and is also used in
[
GV24
]
[
FK24
]
. In these articles, they show that
pull-back
of cohomological correspondence along contracting substacks is compatible with the trace construction. In contrast, we show that
push-forward
of cohomological correspondence along a map admitting contracting boundary is compatible with trace construction, which seems to be new as far as the author knows. This is Theorem
2.27
.

The “compactification” of
\pi:\Bun_{H}\to\Bun_{G}
is a map
\overline{\pi}:\overline{\Bun}_{G}^{X}\to\Bun_{G}
coming from the affine degeneration of the spherical variety
X=H\backslash G
. See §
2.3
for a discussion. This is where the sphericity of
H\subset G
and Assumption
2.36
are used. In the diagonal case
G=H\times H
, this compactification is the famous Drinfeld’s compactification
\overline{\Delta}:\overline{\Bun}_{G}\to\Bun_{G}\times\Bun_{G}
coming from Vinberg’s semigroup. A proof of that such construction gives a compactification (i.e.,
\overline{\Delta}
is proper when
G
is semisimple) is contained in
[
FKM20
, Appendix A]
. For a general homogeneous spherical variety
X=H\backslash G
, the existence of such a compactification seems well-known to the experts, but we cannot find appropriate literature. We prove this by simplifying and generalizing the argument in
loc.cit
, which also yields a shorter proof in the diagonal case. This is Proposition
2.40
.

1.4.
Organization of the article

The organization of this article is as follows:

•

In §
2
, we introduce the preliminaries for this article. In particular, we develop the theory of cohomological correspondence with a kernel that appears ubiquitously in this work.

•

In §
3
, we prove the identity between the special cycle classes and the geometric trace of the cohomological correspondences.

•

In §
4
, we prove the identity between the geometric trace of cohomological correspondences and the classes obtained from the functoriality of categorical trace.

•

In §
5
, we define the
\sigma
-isotypic part in the cohomology of Shtukas and develop general tools to study the isotypic part of special cycle classes.

•

In §
6
, we apply the machinery developed in the previous sections to the Rankin–Selberg case and prove the main result, Theorem
1.7
.

1.5.
Notations and conventions

We now introduce the commonly used notations and conventions in this article.

1.5.1.
Category theory

In this article, by a vector space, we mean a super vector space. By a category, we usually mean an
(\infty,1)
-category. We use
\mathrm{Space}
to denote the
\infty
-category of spaces and
\mathrm{Vect}
to indicate the
\infty
-category of (super) vector spaces. For a category
\mathcal{C}
with a
t
-structure, we use
\mathcal{C}^{\heartsuit}
to denote its heart, which is an abelian category.

For
V\in\mathrm{Vect}
, we define
V\langle 1\rangle=\Pi V[1](1/2)
where

•

\Pi:\mathrm{Vect}\to\mathrm{Vect}
is the functor changing the parity;

•

[1]:\mathrm{Vect}\to\mathrm{Vect}
is the shift functor;

•

(1/2)
is the Tate twist whenever it makes sense (e.g, when working with Frobenius equivariant vector spaces).

For a category
\mathcal{C}
and two objects
x,y\in\mathcal{C}
, we use
\Hom(x,y)\in\mathrm{Space}
to denote the mapping space. We define
\Hom^{0}(x,y)=\pi_{0}(\Hom(x,y))\in\mathrm{Vect}
.

We say a functor between two categories
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is continuous if
L
preserves colimits.

1.5.2.
Sheaf theory

In this article, we work with algebraic stacks, which are Artin stacks locally of finite type over a field
F
. For a algebraic stack
X
over
F
, we use
\mathrm{Shv}(X)=\mathrm{Shv}(X_{\overline{F}})
to denote the category of (ind-constructible) étale sheaves with
k=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-coefficient as introduced in
[
AGK+22c
, Appendix F]
. We use
\mathrm{Shv}(X)_{c}\subset\mathrm{Shv}(X)
to denote the full subcategory of constructible sheaves.

1.5.3.
Geometric notations

In this article, by a
G
-variety
X
, we mean a variety
X
with a
right

G
-action.

We fix a smooth projective curve
C
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. We use
\Bun_{G}=\Map(C,[*/G])
to denote the moduli space of
G
-bundles over
C
. For a smooth affine
G
-variety
X
, we define
\Bun_{G}^{X}:=\Map(C,[X/G])
. It is equipped with a map
\pi:\Bun_{G}^{X}\to\Bun_{G}
which is of finite type. We define the
period sheaf
to be
\mathcal{P}_{X}:=\pi_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}^{X}}
.

We use
I=\{1,2,\cdots,r\}
to denote a finite set that serves as the index set of legs. We use
\Hk_{G,I}
to denote the (iterated) Hecke stack of
G
with
I
-legs, that is, the moduli space of tuples

((c_{i})_{i\in I},\mathcal{E}_{0}{\mathrel{\to}^{c_{1}}}\mathcal{E}_{1}{\mathrel{\to}^{c_{2}}}\cdots{\mathrel{\to}^{c_{r}}}\mathcal{E}_{r})

where

•

c_{i}\in C
for
i\in I
;

•

\mathcal{E}_{i-1}{\mathrel{\to}^{c_{i}}}\mathcal{E}_{i}
is a map between
G
-bundles on
C-\{c_{i}\}
for
i\in I
.

It gives a correspondence

\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}\xleftarrow{\overleftarrow{h}_{I}}\Hk_{G,I}\xrightarrow{\overrightarrow{h}_{I}}\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}

where
\overleftarrow{h}_{I}
sends data above to
(\mathcal{E}_{0},(c_{i})_{i\in I})
and
\overrightarrow{h}_{I}
sends data above to
(\mathcal{E}_{r},(c_{i})_{i\in I})
. We use
l_{I}:\Hk_{G,I}\to C^{I}
to denote the map remembering the legs (sending data above to
(c_{i})_{i\in I}
).

The geometric Satake equivalence attaches each
V^{I}\in\Rep(\check{G}^{I})
a sheaf
\IC_{V^{I}}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G}^{I})
, which is normalized such that when
V^{I}=V_{\lambda_{I}}\in\Rep(\check{G}^{I})^{\heartsuit}
which is irreducible with highest weight
\lambda_{I}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{r})\in X_{*}(T)^{I}
, the sheaf
\IC_{V^{I}}
is perverse, pure of weight
0
, and has the same parity as
\sum_{i\in I}\langle 2\rho,\lambda_{i}\rangle
. We use
\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}
(or
\Hk_{G,V_{\lambda_{I}}}
) to denote the support of
\IC_{V^{I}}
(known as the closed Schubert cell). We use
\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}^{\circ}\subset\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}
to denote the open Schubert cell.

The Hecke operator attached to
V^{I}\in\Rep(\check{G}^{I})
is defined to be the functor

(1.34)

T_{V^{I}}=\overrightarrow{h}_{I,*}(\overleftarrow{h}_{I}^{!}(-)\otimes^{!}\IC_{V^{I}})\cong\overrightarrow{h}_{I,!}(\overleftarrow{h}_{I}^{*}(-)\otimes\IC_{V^{I}}):\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})\to\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}).

We define the moduli space of
G
-Shtukas with
I
-legs to be the stack defined by the fiber product

\hbox to214.85pt{\vbox to52.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 96.7602pt\lower-26.17838pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

We still use

to denote the map remembering the legs. We also have the obvious notion
\Sht_{G,\lambda_{I}}\subset\Sht_{G,I}
(or

).

\Hk_{G,I}^{X}
by adding a rational
X
-section to the data of
\Hk_{G,I}
that is regular for each
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Similarly, we have moduli spaces
\Sht_{G,I}^{X}
,

, and

.

AGK+22b
, Corollary 3.3.7]
, which is one of the starting points of this work. Also, the author would like to thank Tsao-Hsien Chen, Yiannis Sakellaridis, Jinfeng Song, Yakov Varshavsky, and Lingfei Yi for helpful discussions.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce the preliminaries for the article.

•

In §
2.1
, we recall the formalism of categorical trace.

•

In §
2.2
, we develop the theory of cohomological correspondences with kernel.

•

In §
2.3
, we explain the compactification of the map
\pi:\Bun_{G}^{X}\to\Bun_{G}
.

2.1.
Categorical trace

In this section, we recall the formalism of categorical trace. We refer to
[
Zhu25
, §7.3]
for a more detailed treatment.

In the following, by a category, we mean either 1-category, 2-category,
(\infty,1)
-category, or
(\infty,2)
-category.

Definition 2.1

(Categorical trace)
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,1_{\mathcal{C}})
is a symmetric monoidal category. For each dualizable object
x\in\mathcal{C}
and an endomorphism
F\in\End_{\mathcal{C}}(x)
, we define the
categorical trace
of
F
to be the element

\operatorname{tr}(F,x)\in\End_{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}})

defined as the composition

Here,
x^{\vee}
is the dual of
x
,
\mathrm{u}_{x}:1_{\mathcal{C}}\to x\otimes x^{\vee}
and
\ev_{x}:x\otimes x^{\vee}\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}
are the unit and counit map for the duality between
x
and
x^{\vee}
.

When
\mathcal{C}
is a 2-category or
(\infty,2)
-category, the categorical trace admits the following functorial property.

(Functoriality of trace)
.

given by

Suppose we are given
F\in\End_{\mathcal{C}}(x)
,
G\in\End_{\mathcal{C}}(y)
, and a 2-morphism
\eta:L\circ F\to G\circ L
, we define the 2-morphism

\operatorname{tr}(\eta):\operatorname{tr}(F,x)\to\operatorname{tr}(G,y)

to be the 2-morphism from the upper route to the lower route in the diagram

Here the 2-morphism
\gamma
is defined as

\gamma:(L\otimes R^{\vee})\circ\mathrm{u}_{x}=(L\otimes\ev_{x}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ(\mathrm{u}_{x}\otimes R\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\mathrm{u}_{y}\cong((L\circ R)\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\mathrm{u}_{y}\to\mathrm{u}_{y}.

The 2-morphism
\delta
is defined as

\delta:\ev_{x}\to\ev_{x}\circ((R\circ L)\otimes\mathrm{id})\cong\ev_{x}\circ(R\otimes\ev_{y}\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ(\mathrm{u}_{y}\otimes L\otimes\mathrm{id}))=\ev_{y}\circ(L\otimes R^{\vee}).

Example 2.3
.

Consider the symmetric monoidal
(\infty,2)
-category
(\Pr^{L},\otimes,\mathrm{Space})
consisting of presentable
(\infty,1)
-categories with continuous (i.e. colimit preserving) functors. Here
\mathrm{Space}\in\Pr^{L}
is the category of spaces. The construction above assigns to each dualizable presentable
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathcal{C}\in\Pr^{L}
together with a continuous functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
a space
\operatorname{tr}(F,\mathcal{C})\in\End_{\Pr^{L}}(\mathrm{Space})\cong\mathrm{Space}
. Moreover, given another
\mathcal{D}\in\Pr^{L}
equipped with a continuous functor
G:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
, suppose one has a continuous functor
L:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
preserving compact objects (i.e. admits right adjoint
R:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
in
\Pr^{L}
), for each natural transformation
\eta:L\circ F\to G\circ L
, one gets a map between spaces
\operatorname{tr}(\eta):\operatorname{tr}(F,\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{tr}(G,\mathcal{D})
.

Example 2.4
.

For each algebra object
(\mathcal{A},\otimes)\in\Pr^{L}
(i.e. a presentable symmetric monoidal
(\infty,1)
-category such that
\otimes
is continuous), one can consider the
(\infty,2)
-category of linear categories over
\mathcal{A}
defined as
\mathrm{LinCat}_{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathrm{LMod}_{\mathcal{A}}(\Pr^{L})
. This defines a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,2)
-category
(\mathrm{LinCat}_{\mathcal{A}},\otimes_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{A})
. The discussion in Example
2.3
carries over by requiring linearity over
\mathcal{A}
everywhere, and we obtain
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(F,\mathcal{C})\in\End_{\mathrm{LinCat}_{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathcal{A})\cong\mathcal{A}
and map
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\eta):\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(F,\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(G,\mathcal{D})
.

Example 2.5
.

Continuing with Example
2.4
, suppose
\mathcal{A}
is rigid. Then
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{LinCat}_{\mathcal{A}}
is dualizable if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is dualizable as an element in
\Pr^{L}
. The most important cases for us are when
\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{Vect}_{k}
for a field
k
, and
\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})
for a curve
C
over
k
and a finite set
I
. Here, the category
\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})
is defined in
[
AGK+22c
, Definition 1.2.6]
.

2.2.
Cohomological correspondences with kernel

In this section, we develop the theory of cohomological correspondences with kernel. In this section, by an algebraic stack, we mean an Artin stack locally of finite type over a field
F
.

2.2.1.
Definition

Consider a correspondence of algebraic stacks

A_{1}\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A_{0}.

Given
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
.

Definition 2.6
.

For any
\mathcal{F}_{0}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A_{0})
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A_{1})
, a
cohomological correspondence
between
\mathcal{F}_{0}
,
\mathcal{F}_{1}
with kernel
\mathcal{K}
is an element

\mathfrak{c}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1}):=\Hom^{0}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{K},c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F}_{1}).

Remark 2.7
.

When
\mathcal{K}=\underline{k}_{C}\langle-d\rangle
for
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, this is the usual notion of cohomological correspondence of degree
d
.

Suppose the right square of (
2.2
) is pushable (i.e., the map
C\to A_{0}\times_{B_{0}}D
is proper). Given
\mathcal{F}_{i}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A_{i})
for
i=0,1
,
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
and
\mathcal{L}\in\mathrm{Shv}(D)
together with a map
\alpha:f^{*}\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{K}
, one can define the
push-forward
of cohomological correspondence which is a map

f_{!}:\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})\to\mathrm{Corr}_{D,\mathcal{L}}(f_{A_{0},!}\mathcal{F}_{0},f_{A_{1},!}\mathcal{F}_{1})

defined such that for any
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})=\Hom^{0}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{K},c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F}_{1})
, we have

f_{!}\mathfrak{c}:d_{0}^{*}f_{A_{0},!}\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{L}\to f_{!}c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}f_{!}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L})\to f_{!}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{K})\to f_{!}c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F}_{1}\to d_{1}^{!}f_{A_{1},!}\mathcal{F}_{1}.

Here, the first map uses the base change map for pushable square (see
[
FYZ23
, §3.2]
), the second map uses projection formula for
f
, the third map uses
\alpha:f^{*}\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{K}
, the fourth map uses the map
\mathfrak{c}
, the final map is the Beck-Chevalley base change map for the left square of (
2.2
).

The condition that the right square is pushable is equivalent to requiring
f
to be proper. Note that
\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\underline{k}_{S},\underline{k}_{S})=H_{0}^{BM}(C/S,\mathcal{K})=\Hom^{0}(\mathcal{K},\omega_{C/S})
and a similar statement holds for
D
. The push-forward of cohomological correspondence gives for each
\alpha:f^{*}\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{K}
a map

f_{!}:H_{0}^{BM}(C/S,\mathcal{K})\to H_{0}^{BM}(D/S,\mathcal{L}).

In particular, when
\mathcal{K}=\underline{k}_{C}
,
\mathcal{L}=\underline{k}_{D}
, and
\alpha
is the natural map
f^{*}\underline{k}_{D}\to\underline{k}_{C}
, this gives the usual proper push-forward of Borel-Moore homology class.

2.2.3.
Pull-back

In this section, we switch to derived algebraic geometry and consider derived algebraic stacks. For applications in this article, one can safely work within classical algebraic geometry and pretend all the quasi-smooth maps involved are smooth. Consider the same diagram (
2.2
) but assume the left square is pullable of defect
d
(i.e., the map
C\to A_{1}\times_{B_{1}}D
is quasi-smooth of relative dimension
d
). Given
\mathcal{F}_{i}\in\mathrm{Shv}(B_{i})
for
i=0,1
,
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(D)
and
\mathcal{L}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
together with a map
\alpha:\mathcal{L}\langle-2d\rangle\to f^{*}\mathcal{K}
, one can define the
pull-back
of cohomological correspondence which is a map

f^{*}:\mathrm{Corr}_{D,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})\to\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{L}}(f^{*}_{A_{0}}\mathcal{F}_{0},f^{*}_{A_{1}}\mathcal{F}_{1})

defined such that for any
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{D,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})=\Hom^{0}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{K},c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F}_{1})
, we have

f^{*}\mathfrak{c}:(c_{0}^{*}f_{A_{0}}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0})\otimes\mathcal{L}\to f^{*}d_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{K}\langle 2d\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}f^{*}(d_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{K})\langle 2d\rangle\to f^{*}d_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F}_{1}\langle 2d\rangle\to c_{1}^{!}f_{A_{1}}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{1}.

Here, the first maps uses
\alpha
, the third map uses
\mathfrak{c}
, the last map uses the base change map
f^{*}d_{1}^{!}\langle 2d\rangle\to c_{1}^{!}f_{A_{1}}^{*}
for pull-back square of defect
d
(see
[
FYZ23
, §3.5]
).

Example 2.9

(Pull-back of Borel-Moore homology class)
.

Consider diagram (
2.3
). The condition that the left square is pullable of defect
d
is equivalent to requiring
f
to be quasi-smooth of relative dimension
d
. The pull-back of cohomological correspondence gives for each
\alpha:\mathcal{L}\langle-2d\rangle\to f^{*}\mathcal{K}
a map

f^{*}:H_{0}^{BM}(D/S,\mathcal{K})\to H_{0}^{BM}(C/S,\mathcal{L}).

When
\mathcal{K}=\underline{k}_{D}
,
\mathcal{L}=\underline{k}_{C}\langle 2d\rangle
, and
\alpha
is the natural map
\underline{k}_{C}\to f^{*}\underline{k}_{D}
, this gives the usual pull-back of Borel-Moore homology class along a quasi-smooth map.

2.2.4.
Specialization

In this section, we use
\eta
to denote the generic point of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{F}
and
s=0\in\mathbb{A}^{1}_{F}(F)
.
For any algebraic stack
A
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{F}
, one has the nearby cycle functor
\Psi:\mathrm{Shv}(A_{\eta})\to\mathrm{Shv}(A_{s})
.

Consider a cohomological correspondence over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{F}
:

A_{1}\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A_{0}.

Given
\mathcal{F}_{i}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A_{i,\eta})
for
i=0,1
,
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C_{\eta})
,
\mathcal{L}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C_{s})
together with a morphism
\alpha:\mathcal{L}\to\Psi\mathcal{K}
, one can define the
specialization
of cohomological correspondence to be the map

\Psi:\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{\eta},\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})\to\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{s},\mathcal{L}}(\Psi\mathcal{F}_{0},\Psi\mathcal{F}_{1})

defined such that for any
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{\eta},\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
one has

\Psi\mathfrak{c}:(c_{0,s}^{*}\Psi\mathcal{F}_{0})\otimes\mathcal{L}\to\Psi(c_{0,\eta}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0})\otimes\Psi\mathcal{K}\to\Psi(c_{0,\eta}^{*}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{K})\xrightarrow{\Psi(\mathfrak{c})}\Psi c_{1,\eta}^{!}\mathcal{F}_{1}\to c_{1,s}^{!}\Psi\mathcal{F}_{1}.

Example 2.10

(Specialization of Borel-Moore homology class)
.

Suppose the correspondence has the form

S\xleftarrow{c}C\xrightarrow{c}S.

The construction above defines for each
\alpha:\mathcal{L}\to\Psi\mathcal{K}
a map

\Psi:H_{0}^{BM}(C_{\eta}/S_{\eta},\mathcal{K})\to H_{0}^{BM}(C_{s}/S_{s},\mathcal{L}),

which coincides with the usual specialization of Borel-Moore homology class when
\mathcal{K}=\underline{k}_{C_{\eta}}
,
\mathcal{L}=\underline{k}_{C_{s}}
, and
\alpha:\Psi\underline{k}_{C_{\eta}}\to\underline{k}_{C_{s}}
is the natural map.

2.2.5.
Geometric trace construction

In the following, we work with algebraic stacks over a base algebraic stack
S
. For any such stack
A
, we define the category
\mathrm{Shv}(A)_{\mathrm{ULA}}\subset\mathrm{Shv}(A)
to be the full subcategory of sheaves that are ULA over
S
. We use
\mathbb{D}_{S}:\mathrm{Shv}(A)_{\mathrm{ULA}}\to\mathrm{Shv}(A)_{\mathrm{ULA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
to denote the relative Verdier duality functor.

Consider a self-correspondence between algebraic stacks
(A\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A)
with a kernel
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
. Given
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A)_{\mathrm{ULA}}

. Consider Cartesian diagram
