Definition 2.11
.

We define the
geometric trace
of cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}
to be the element

\operatorname{tr}_{S}(\mathfrak{c})\in H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C)/S,\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)})=\Hom^{0}(\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)},\omega_{\Fix(C)/S})

given by the composition

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)}&=i^{*}\mathcal{K}\\
&\xrightarrow{i^{*}\mathfrak{c}}i^{*}\underline{\Hom}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F},c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}i^{*}(c_{1},c_{0})^{!}\underline{\Hom}(\pr_{2}^{*}\mathcal{F},\pr_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F})\\
&\to p^{!}\Delta^{*}(\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{F})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}p^{!}(\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F})\otimes\mathcal{F})\\
&\to p^{!}\omega_{A/S}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\omega_{\Fix(C)/S}\end{split}.

The third row uses isomorphism
\underline{\Hom}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F},c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F})\cong(c_{1},c_{0})^{!}\underline{\Hom}(\pr_{2}^{*}\mathcal{F},\pr_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F})
, the fourth row uses base change map
i^{*}(c_{1}\times c_{0})^{!}\to p^{!}\Delta^{*}
and the natural isomorphism
\underline{\Hom}(\pr_{2}^{*}\mathcal{F},\pr_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F})\cong\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{F}
, the sixth line uses
\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F})\otimes\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{A/S}
.

Remark 2.12
.

When
\mathcal{K}=\underline{k}_{C}
, the definition above coincides with the definition in
[
FYZ23
, §4.1]
.

Remark 2.13
.

We call the above construction a geometric trace to distinguish it from the categorical trace considered in other parts of the article. Note that the diagram (
2.4
) can not be obtained from the diagram (
2.1
) by taking
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{LinCat}_{\mathrm{Shv}(S)}
,
x=\mathrm{Shv}(A)
, and
y=\mathrm{Shv}(S)
due to the failure of the categorical Künneth formula. There are two ways to understand the geometric trace via categorical trace: It can be either understood as a categorical trace in Lu–Zheng’s category, as in §
2.2.10
, or as a functoriality of categorical trace in the geometric Langlands setting by putting a nilpotent singular support condition which remedies the categorical Künneth formula.

2.2.6.
Geometric trace as a natural transformation

Now we give another description of the geometric trace

\operatorname{tr}_{S}(\mathfrak{c})\in\Hom^{0}(\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)},\omega_{\Fix(C)/S})\cong\Hom^{0}(f_{\Fix(C),!}\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)},\underline{k}_{S}).

Here we use
f_{\Fix(C)}:\Fix(C)\to S
to denote the structural morphism of
\Fix(C)
. We use
f:A\to S
(resp,
f_{2}:A\times_{S}A\to S
) to denote the structural morphism of
A
(resp.
A\times_{S}A
).

Consider correspondence
(A\times_{S}A\xleftarrow{c_{1}\times\mathrm{id}}C\times_{S}A\xrightarrow{c_{0}\times\mathrm{id}}A\times_{S}A)
and the map
\pr_{1}:C\times_{S}A\to C
which is projection onto the first coordinate. We have the lax commutative diagram

(2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(A\times_{S}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(A\times_{S}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{!}f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c_{0}\times\mathrm{id})_{!}(\pr_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}\otimes(c_{1}\times\mathrm{id})^{*}(-))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2,!}(-\otimes\mathcal{F}\boxtimes_{S}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathfrak{c}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2,!}(-\otimes\mathcal{F}\boxtimes_{S}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}\Delta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathcal{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

We now explain the three natural transformations in (
2.4
):

•

The natural transformation
\gamma_{\mathcal{F}}
is defined by

\gamma_{\mathcal{F}}:f_{2,!}(\Delta_{!}f^{*}(-)\otimes\mathcal{F}\boxtimes_{S}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}f_{2,!}\Delta_{!}(f^{*}(-)\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}))\to f_{!}(f^{*}(-)\otimes\omega_{A/S})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}-\otimes f_{!}f^{!}\underline{k}_{S}\to\mathrm{id}.

The first map is given by the projection formula for
\Delta
, the second map is given by
f_{2,!}\Delta_{!}=f_{!}
and
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F})\to\omega_{A/S}
, the third map is given by the projection formula for
f
, the final map is given by the adjunction map for adjoint pair
(f_{!},f^{!})
.

•

Consider the cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C\times_{S}A,\mathcal{K}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{A}}(\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{A},\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{A})
. The natural transformation
\eta_{\mathfrak{c}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}
is given by

(2.5)

\begin{split}\eta_{\mathfrak{c}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}:f_{2,!}((c_{0}\times\mathrm{id})_{!}(\pr_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}\otimes(c_{1}\times\mathrm{id})^{*}(-))\otimes\mathcal{F}\boxtimes_{S}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}))&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}f_{2,!}(-\otimes c_{1,!}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{K})\boxtimes_{S}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}))\\
&\to f_{2,!}(-\otimes\mathcal{F}\boxtimes_{S}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}))\end{split}.

The first isomorphism comes from several applications of base change isomorphisms and projection formula, the second map comes from the cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}:c_{1,!}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{K})\to\mathcal{F}
. Note that the middle square in (
2.4
) is induced from the following more primitive square:

(2.6)

•

The natural transformation
\delta_{\mathcal{F}}
is given by

\delta_{\mathcal{F}}:f_{!}\Delta^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}f_{2,!}(-\otimes\Delta_{!}\underline{k}_{A})\to f_{2,!}(-\otimes\Delta_{!}(\mathcal{F}\otimes^{!}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F})))\to f_{2,!}(-\otimes\mathcal{F}\boxtimes_{S}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F})).

The first isomorphism uses the projection formula for
\Delta
, the second map uses the map
\underline{k}_{A}\to\mathcal{F}\otimes^{!}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F})
, the third map uses the adjunction map for adjoint pair
(\Delta_{!},\Delta^{!})
.

Note that after evaluating two routes from the left-top corner of (
2.4
) to the right-bottom corner of (
2.4
) on the element
\underline{k}_{S}\in\mathrm{Shv}(S)
, we get a map

(2.7)

\operatorname{tr}^{\prime}_{S}(\mathfrak{c}):f_{\Fix(C),!}\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)}\cong f_{!}\Delta^{*}(c_{0}\times\mathrm{id})_{!}(\pr_{1}^{*}\mathcal{K}\otimes(c_{1}\times\mathrm{id})^{*}\Delta_{!}f^{*}\underline{k}_{S})\to\underline{k}_{S}.

Lemma 2.14
.

We have
\operatorname{tr}^{\prime}_{S}(\mathfrak{c})=\operatorname{tr}_{S}(\mathfrak{c})\in\Hom^{0}(f_{\Fix(C),!}\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)},\underline{k}_{S})
.

Proof.

This is a routine diagram chase. We leave it to the reader.
∎

2.2.7.
Geometric Shtuka construction

In this section, we assume the base field
F=\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

Definition 2.15
.

For each correspondence
c:(A\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A)
, we define its
Frobenius twist
to be the correspondence
c^{(1)}:(A\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{\Frob\circ c_{0}}A)
. For each cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
, we define

\mathfrak{c}^{(1)}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})

5
5

5

Note that the two vector spaces
\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
appearing here are different: The first is defined using
c
and the second is defined using
c^{(1)}
.

to be the element

\mathfrak{c}^{(1)}:(c_{0}^{*}\circ\Frob^{*}\mathcal{F})\otimes\mathcal{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F})\otimes\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{c}}c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F}.

2.2.8.
Functoriality of geometric trace I

Now we formulate compatibilities of the geometric trace construction in §
2.2.5
with proper push-forward, smooth pull-back, and specialization. We will leave their proof to §
2.2.10
.

Theorem 2.16

(Compatibility with proper push-forward)
.

Consider a morphism of correspondences between algebraic stacks over
S

(2.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

in which
f_{A},f
are proper
6
6

6

By a proper morphism between algebraic stacks, we mean a map representable in proper algebraic spaces.
(hence the right square is pushable). Then the induced map
\Fix(f):\Fix(C)\to\Fix(D)
is proper.
Given
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A)_{\mathrm{ULA}}
and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
, together with a map
\alpha:f^{*}\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{K}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{D,\mathcal{L}}(f_{A,!}\mathcal{F},f_{A,!}\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C)/S,\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(D)/S,\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(D)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fix(f)_{!}}

is commutative. Here the map
\Fix(f)_{!}:H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C)/S,\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)})\to H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(D)/S,\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(D)})
is induced from the map

\Fix(\alpha):\Fix(f)^{*}(\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(D)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(f^{*}\mathcal{L})|_{\Fix(C)}\xrightarrow{\alpha|_{\Fix(C)}}\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)}

via Example
2.8
.

Theorem 2.17

(Compatibility with smooth pull-back)
.

Consider the diagram (
2.8
). Assume
f_{A}
is smooth and the left square is pullable of defect
d
. Then the induced map
\Fix(f):\Fix(C)\to\Fix(D)
is quasi-smooth of relative dimension
d
. Given
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(B)_{\mathrm{ULA}}
,
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(D)
,
\mathcal{L}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
, and a map
\alpha:\mathcal{L}\langle-2d\rangle\to f^{*}\mathcal{K}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{D,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{L}}(f_{A}^{*}\mathcal{F},f_{A}^{*}\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(D)/S,\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(D)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C)/S,\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(C)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fix(f)^{*}}

is commutative. Here the map
\Fix(f)^{*}:H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(D)/S,\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(D)})\to H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C)/S,\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(C)})
is induced from the map

\Fix(\alpha):\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(C)}\langle-2d\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha|_{\Fix(C)}}(f^{*}\mathcal{K})|_{\Fix(C)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Fix(f)^{*}(\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(D)})

via Example
2.9
.

Recall we use
\eta
to denote the generic point of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{F}
and
s
to denote the zero point of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{F}
.

Theorem 2.18

(Compatibility with specialization)
.

Consider an algebraic stack
S
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{F}
. Given a correspondence between algebraic stacks over
S
:

A\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A,

together with sheaves
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A_{\eta})_{\mathrm{ULA}}
,
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C_{\eta})
,
\mathcal{L}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C_{s})
, and a map
\alpha:\mathcal{L}\to\Psi\mathcal{K}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{\eta},\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{s},\mathcal{L}}(\Psi\mathcal{F},\Psi\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C_{\eta})/S_{\eta},\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C_{\eta})})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C_{s})/S_{s},\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(C_{s})})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S_{\eta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S_{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

is commutative. Here the map
\Psi:H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C_{\eta})/S_{\eta},\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C_{\eta})})\to H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C_{s})/S_{s},\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(C_{s})})
is induced from the map

\Fix(\alpha):\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(C_{s})}\to(\Psi\mathcal{K})|_{\Fix(C_{s})}\to\Psi(\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C_{\eta})})

via Example
2.10
.

Remark 2.19
.

Note that theorems above with constant kernel were proved in
[
Var07
]
and
[
LZ22
]
by slightly different methods for schemes. The argument of
[
LZ22
]
can be easily generalized to Artin stacks (see
[
FK24
]
). We will also prove these theorems by generalizing the argument of
[
LZ22
]
.

2.2.9.
Functoriality of geometric trace II

Now we generalize Theorem
2.16
to the case that
f
admits compactification with contracting boundary. The main result in this section is Theorem
2.27
.

Our strategy follows
[
Var07
]
[
GV24
]
[
FK24
]
, in which they prove compatibilities of geometric trace with restriction to a closed substack on which the correspondence is contracting in different settings. As they did in
loc.cit
, we use deformation to the normal cone, which has very simple behavior along a substack on which the correspondence is contracting.

We begin with the definition of a correspondence contracting on a substack following
[
Var07
, Definition 1.5.1]
(in the scheme case) and
[
FK24
, Definition 7.2.1]
(for Artin stacks):

Definition 2.20
.

Consider a correspondence between algebraic stacks over
S

c:(A\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A).

Let
Z\subset A
be a closed substack defined by an ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{Z}\subset\mathcal{O}_{A}
.

•

We say that
c
stabilizes
Z
if
c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{Z}\subset c_{1}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{Z}
.

•

We say that
c
is
contracting near

Z
if
c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{Z}\subset c_{1}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{Z}
and there exists
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{Z}^{n}\subset c_{1}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{Z}^{n+1}
.

Example 2.21
.

Given a correspondence between algebraic stacks
c=(A\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A)
defined over
S/\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Then
c^{(1)}
is contracting on
Z
if
c
stabilizes
Z
.

For
Z
stabilized by
c
, denote
C_{Z}:=C\times_{A}Z
in which we are using
c_{1}:C\to A
. Consider the correspondence

D_{Z}(A)\xleftarrow{\widetilde{c}_{1}}D_{C_{Z}}(C)\xrightarrow{\widetilde{c}_{0}}D_{Z}(A)

where
D_{Z}(A)
is the deformation to the normal cone of
A
along the closed substack
Z
and similarly for
D_{C_{Z}}(C)
. This correspondence is defined over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
, whose fiber over any
S
-point of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
which is disjoint from zero is identical to
(A\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A)
, while the fiber over
0\in\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
is identified with

N_{Z}(A)\xleftarrow{N(c_{1})}N_{C_{Z}}(C)\xrightarrow{N(c_{0})}N_{Z}(A)

where
N_{Z}(A)
is the normal cone of
A
along
Z
and similarly for
N_{C_{Z}}(C)
.

The following are some easy facts:

Lemma 2.22
.

Consider a correspondence
c
between algebraic stacks over
S

A\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A

with a closed substack
Z\subset A
on which
c
is contracting. The following statements are true:

(1)

The set-theoretic image of
N(c_{0}):N_{C_{Z}}(C)\to N_{Z}(A)
is contained in
Z\subset N_{Z}(A)
.

(2)

The natural map defines an open and closed embedding
\Fix(C_{Z})_{\mathrm{red}}\subset\Fix(C)_{\mathrm{red}}
.

(3)

The natural map defines an isomorphism
\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))_{\mathrm{red}}\cong\Fix(C_{Z})_{\mathrm{red}}
.

(4)

We have disjoint decomposition
\Fix(D_{C_{Z}}(C))_{\mathrm{red}}=\Fix(C_{Z})_{\mathrm{red}}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\coprod(\Fix(C)_{\mathrm{red}}\backslash\Fix(C_{Z})_{\mathrm{red}})\times_{S}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\backslash\{0\})
.

Proof.

(1) is
[
FK24
, Lemma 7.2.4]
. (2) is
[
FK24
, Proposition 7.5.4]
. (3) is a consequence of (1). (4) is
[
FK24
, Proposition 7.5.5]
.
7
7

7

The formulation in
loc.cit
is not correct as stated. The proof in
loc.cit
shows what we state here.

∎

In this case, we call the nearby cycle functors
\Psi:\mathrm{Shv}(A)\to\mathrm{Shv}(N_{Z}(A))
and
\Psi:\mathrm{Shv}(C)\to\mathrm{Shv}(N_{C_{Z}}(C))
specializations.

Note the following property of the
\Psi
:

Lemma 2.23
.

The natural transformation
\alpha:i_{Z,0}^{*}\circ\Psi\to i_{Z}^{*}
is an isomorphism. Here
i_{Z,0}:Z\to N_{Z}(A)
is the zero section,
i_{Z}:Z\to A
is the natural inclusion.

Proof.

This is
[
GV24
, §7.5(b)]
.
∎

The following is a direct consequence of Theorem
2.18
:

Proposition 2.24
.

Consider a correspondence
c
between algebraic stacks over
S

A\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A

with a closed substack
Z\subset A
stabilized by
c
. For
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A)_{\mathrm{ULA}}
and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{N_{C_{Z}}(C),\Psi\mathcal{K}}(\Psi\mathcal{F},\Psi\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C)/S,\mathcal{K}_{\Fix(C)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))/S,(\Psi\mathcal{K})|_{\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

is commutative.

We have the following corollary which is generalization of
[
GV24
, Corollary 5.6(c)]
:

Corollary 2.25
.

In the setting of Proposition
2.24
, assume moreover that
c
is contracting along
Z
. If
\mathcal{F}|_{Z}=0
, then the composition

\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{tr}_{S}}H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C),\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)})\xrightarrow{i_{C_{Z}}^{*}}H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C_{Z}),\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C_{Z})})

is zero. Here, the map
i_{C_{Z}}:\Fix(C_{Z})\subset\Fix(C)
is an open and closed embedding on the reduced substacks by Lemma
2.22
(2) and
i_{C_{Z}}^{*}
is the natural restriction map.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
2.23
to
C_{Z}\subset C
and using Lemma
2.22
(3), we get a natural isomorphism

(\Psi\mathcal{K})|_{\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))}\cong\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C_{Z})}.

Under this isomorphism, one can check that the restriction map

i_{C_{Z}}^{*}:H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C)/S,\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)})\to H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C_{Z})/S,\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C_{Z})})

is identified with the map

\Psi:H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C)/S,\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)})\to H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))/S,(\Psi\mathcal{K})|_{\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))})

used in Proposition
2.24
. By Proposition
2.24
, we are reduced to show that the composition

\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\Psi}\mathrm{Corr}_{N_{C_{Z}}(C),\Psi\mathcal{K}}(\Psi\mathcal{F},\Psi\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{tr}_{S}}H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))/S,(\Psi\mathcal{K})|_{\Fix(N_{C_{Z}}(C))})

is zero. Note that

\mathrm{Corr}_{N_{C_{Z}}(C),\Psi\mathcal{K}}(\Psi\mathcal{F},\Psi\mathcal{F})=\Hom^{0}(N(c_{0})^{*}\Psi\mathcal{F}\otimes\Psi\mathcal{K},N(c_{1})^{!}\Psi\mathcal{F}).

By Lemma
2.22
(1) and Lemma
2.23
, we know

N(c_{0})^{*}\Psi\mathcal{F}\cong N(c_{0})^{*}i_{Z,0,!}i_{Z,0}^{*}\Psi\mathcal{F}\cong N(c_{0})^{*}i_{Z,0,!}i_{Z}^{*}\mathcal{F}=0.

This implies
\mathrm{Corr}_{N_{C_{Z}}(C),\Psi\mathcal{K}}(\Psi\mathcal{F},\Psi\mathcal{F})=0
and we are done.
∎

.

:

•

We say that
f

admits compactification with contracting boundary
if the diagram can be extended to a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{c}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{c}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

in which
j_{A}
and
j
are open immersions,
f_{\overline{A}},\overline{f}
are proper, the two top squares are Cartesian, and there exists an ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{Z}\subset\mathcal{O}_{A}
defining a closed substack
Z\subset\overline{A}
on which the middle correspondence
\overline{c}
is contracting and
Z_{\mathrm{red}}=\partial A:=\overline{A}\backslash A
.

•

We say that
f

admits compactification
if the condition above still holds except we only require
Z
to be stabilized by
\overline{c}
.

•

For
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A)_{\mathrm{ULA}}
and a compactification as above, we say that
\mathcal{F}
is
good
for the compactification if
j_{A,!}\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\overline{A})_{\mathrm{ULA}}
.

Theorem 2.27
.

In the setting of Theorem
2.16
but only requiring that
f
admits compactification with contracting boundary and
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A)_{\mathrm{ULA}}
is good for the the compactification, then the map
\Fix(f):\Fix(C)\to\Fix(D)
is proper, and the same conclusion holds.

Proof.

The conclusion that
\Fix(f)
is proper follows from Lemma
2.22
(2).
We can assume
f^{*}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{K}
since the general situation can be deduced from this case. Consider the diagram

(2.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{C,f^{*}\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{\overline{C},\overline{f}^{*}\mathcal{L}}(j_{A,!}\mathcal{F},j_{A,!}\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{D,\mathcal{L}}(f_{A,!}\mathcal{F},f_{A,!}\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(C)/S,f^{*}\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(C)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(\overline{C})/S,\overline{f}^{*}\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(\overline{C})})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Fix(D)/S,\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(D)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fix(j)_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fix(\overline{f})_{!}}

in which each horizontal map is induced by a push-forward of cohomological correspondence. We want to show that the outer square of (
2.9
) is commutative. Theorem
2.16
implies that the right square is commutative. Therefore, we only need to show the commutativity of the left square.

Define
\partial\overline{C}=\overline{C}\backslash C
and
i_{\partial C}:\partial C\to C
. Consider the diagram

(2.10)

By Theorem
2.17
, the left square of (
2.10
) is commutative.
Note that the horizontal maps in the left square of (
2.9
) are splittings of the horizontal maps in the left square of (
2.10
) and
\Fix(i_{\partial C})^{*}\circ\Fix(j)_{!}=0
by Lemma
2.22
(2). Therefore, we are reduced to show
\Fix(i_{\partial C})^{*}\circ\operatorname{tr}_{S}\circ j_{!}=0
. This follows from Corollary
2.25
since
(j_{A,!}\mathcal{F})|_{\partial A}=0
.
∎

We have the following immediate corollary of Theorem
2.27
and Example
2.21
:

Corollary 2.28
.

2.2.10.
Lu–Zheng category

In this section, we follow the strategy in
[
LZ22
]
to prove theorems in §
2.2.8
. We consider variants of
2
-categories considered in
[
LZ22
]
adapted to cohomological correspondences with kernel. Note that similar construction is also made in
[
FK24
]
.

Definition 2.29
.

Let
S
be an algebraic stack. We define the symmetric monoidal
2
-category

(\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!},\otimes,1_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}})

as follows:

•

The objects are pairs
(A,\mathcal{F})
where
A
is an algebraic stack over
S
, and
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A)
.

•

A morphism from
(A_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})
to
(A_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})
is a triple
(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})
where
c=(A_{1}\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A_{0})
is a correspondence,
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
is a kernel sheaf, and
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
is a cohomological correspondence with kernel
\mathcal{K}
.

•

The composition of morphisms
(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}):(A_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})\to(A_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})
and
(d,\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{d}):(A_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})\to(A_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2})
is
(e,\mathcal{M},\mathfrak{e})
, where
e
is the outer correspondence in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}

where the diamond is Cartesian, the kernel
\mathcal{M}=d_{0}^{\prime*}\mathcal{K}\otimes c_{1}^{\prime*}\mathcal{L}
, and
\mathfrak{e}=\mathfrak{d}\circ\mathfrak{c}
is the obvious notion of composition of cohomological correspondences.

•

Given two morphisms
(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})
and
(d,\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{d})
from
(A_{0},\mathcal{K}_{0})
to
(A_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})
, a
2
-morphism
\eta:(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})\to(d,\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{d})
is a map of correspondences

(2.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

in which
f
is proper, together with a map
\alpha:f^{*}\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{K}
such that
f_{!}\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{d}
.

•

The monoidal unit
1_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}}=(S,\underline{k}_{S})
. The tensor product of objects
(A_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})
and
(A_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})
is defined as

(A_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})\otimes(A_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1}):=(A_{0}\times_{S}A_{1},\mathcal{F}_{0}\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{F}_{1}).

The tensor product of morphisms is defined verbatim as in
[
FK24
, §5.1.3]
.

Consider the subcategory
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}^{0}
with the same objects, whose
1
-morphisms are those
1
-morphisms
(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})
in
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}
with
\mathcal{K}=\underline{k}_{C}
, and whose
2
-morphisms are those
(f,\alpha)
as above such that
\alpha:f^{*}\underline{k}_{D}\to\underline{k}_{C}
is the tautological map. Then
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}^{0}
is the original
2
-category considered in
[
LZ22
]
(but considering algebraic stacks instead of schemes) and
[
FK24
]
(but working with étale sheaf theory rather than motivic sheaf theory).

Lemma 2.30
.

Any object
(A,\mathcal{F})
such that
\mathcal{F}
is ULA over
S
is dualizable in
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}
, and whose dual is given by
(A,\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}))
.

Proof.

The above statement already holds in the subcategory
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}^{0}\subset\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}
by
[
LZ22
, Theorem 2.16]
and any dualizable object in
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}^{0}
is dualizable in
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}
.
∎

The following can be checked directly:

Lemma 2.31
.

For a morphism
(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}):(A_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})\to(A_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})
in which
\mathcal{F}_{i}
are ULA over
S
for
i=0,1
, we have
(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})^{\vee}=(c^{\prime},\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}^{\vee})
in which
c^{\prime}=(A_{0}\xleftarrow{c_{0}}C\xrightarrow{c_{1}}A_{1})
and
\mathfrak{c}^{\vee}
is the image of
\mathfrak{c}
under the isomorphism

\begin{split}\Hom^{0}(c_{0}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{K},c_{1}^{!}\mathcal{F}_{1})&\cong\Hom^{0}(\mathcal{K},(c_{1},c_{0})^{!}(\mathcal{F}_{1}\boxtimes_{S}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}_{0}))\\
&\cong\Hom^{0}(\mathcal{K},(c_{0},c_{1})^{!}(\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}_{0})\boxtimes_{S}\mathcal{F}_{1}))\\
&\cong\Hom^{0}(c_{1}^{*}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}_{1})\otimes\mathcal{K},c_{0}^{!}\mathbb{D}_{S}(\mathcal{F}_{0}))\end{split}

Lemma 2.32
.

For a proper map
f:A\to B
and
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A)
, consider correspondence
c_{f}=(B\xleftarrow{f}A=A)
and cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}_{f}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{A,\underline{k}_{A}}(\mathcal{F},f_{!}\mathcal{F})
given by the natural adjunction map
\mathcal{F}\to f^{!}f_{!}\mathcal{F}
. Then the map

(c_{f},\underline{k}_{A},\mathfrak{c}_{f}):(A,\mathcal{F})\to(B,f_{!}\mathcal{F})

admits right adjoint

(c_{f}^{\prime},\underline{k}_{A},\mathfrak{c}_{f}^{\prime}):(B,f_{!}\mathcal{F})\to(A,\mathcal{F})

in which
c_{f}^{\prime}=(A=A\xrightarrow{f}B)
and
\mathfrak{c}_{f}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{A,\underline{k}_{A}}(f_{!}\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
is the natural map
f^{*}f_{!}\mathcal{F}\cong f^{*}f_{*}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

The happens already in
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}^{0}
by
[
LZ22
, Lemma 2.9]
.
∎

Note that the
1
-category
\End_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}}(1_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}})
has its objects consisting of triples
(A,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})
where
A
is an algebraic stack over
S
,
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(A)
, and
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{A,\mathcal{K}}(\underline{k}_{S},\underline{k}_{S})=H^{BM}_{0}(A/S,\mathcal{K})
. For any pair
(A,\mathcal{F})\in\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}
such that
\mathcal{F}
is ULA over
S
together with an endomorphism
(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})\in\End_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}}(A,\mathcal{F})
, applying the categorical trace construction in §
2.1
, one gets an object

\operatorname{tr}((c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}),(A,\mathcal{F}))\in\End_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}}(1_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}}).

The following follows from unwinding definition:

Proposition 2.33
.

We have
\operatorname{tr}((c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}),(A,\mathcal{F}))=(\Fix(C),\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)},\operatorname{tr}_{S}(\mathfrak{c}))\in\End_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}}(1_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}})
.

.

To define the
2
-morphism
\eta
, consider the map between correspondence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\times_{B}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\,c_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{2}}

in which
a:D\times_{B}A\to B
is induced from the map
d_{1}:D\to B
. Note that
(f,c_{0})
is proper by the pushability assumption. Consider the map
(f,c_{0})^{*}\pr_{1}^{*}\mathcal{L}\cong f^{*}\mathcal{L}\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathcal{K}
. Unwinding the definition of push-forward of cohomological correspondence in §
2.2.2
, we know
(f,c_{0})_{!}(\mathfrak{c}_{f}\circ\mathfrak{c})=f_{!}\mathfrak{c}\circ\mathfrak{c}_{f}
. Therefore, the data above defines a
2
-morphism
\eta
.

By Lemma
2.30
and Lemma
2.32
, we can apply the functoriality of categorical trace Definition
2.2
to obtain a
2
-morphism

\operatorname{tr}(\eta):(\Fix(C),\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)},\operatorname{tr}_{S}(\mathfrak{c}))\to(\Fix(D),\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(D)},\operatorname{tr}_{S}(f_{!}\mathfrak{c})).

Using Lemma
2.31
and unwinding the construction of Definition
2.2
, we know that the natural transformation
\operatorname{tr}(\eta)
contains the data
\Fix(\alpha):\Fix(f)^{*}(\mathcal{L}|_{\Fix(D)})\to\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)}
. This forces
\Fix(f)_{!}\operatorname{tr}_{S}(\mathfrak{c})=\operatorname{tr}_{S}(f_{!}\mathfrak{c})
and we are done.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.17
.

One can define a
2
-category
\mathrm{LZ}(S)^{*}
which has the same objects and
1
-morphisms as
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}
but with
2
-morphism given by the same diagram of correspondence (
2.11
) and kernel but with
f
quasi-smooth of some dimension
d
together with a map
\alpha:\mathcal{L}\langle-2d\rangle\to\mathcal{K}
such that
f^{*}\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{c}
. Then Lemma
2.30
, Lemma
2.31
, and Proposition
2.33
still hold in this case. The analogue of Lemma
2.32
is the following easy lemma:

Lemma 2.34
.

For a smooth map
f:A\to B
of relative dimension
d
and
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(B)
, consider correspondence
c_{f}^{\prime}=(A=A\xrightarrow{f}B)
and the tautological cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}_{f}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{A,\underline{k}_{A}}(\mathcal{F},f^{*}\mathcal{F})
. The map

(c_{f}^{\prime},\underline{k}_{A},\mathfrak{c}_{f}^{\prime}):(B,\mathcal{F})\to(A,f^{*}\mathcal{F})

admits right adjoint

(c_{f},\underline{k}_{A}\langle 2d\rangle,\mathfrak{c}_{f}):(A,f^{*}\mathcal{F})\to(B,\mathcal{F})

in which
\mathfrak{c}_{f}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{A,\underline{k}_{A}\langle 2d\rangle}(f^{*}\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
is the natural map
f^{*}\mathcal{F}\langle 2d\rangle\cong f^{!}\mathcal{F}
.

The rest of the proof remains the same as the proper push-forward case.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.18
.

One can easily reduce to the case
\mathcal{L}=\Psi\mathcal{K}
. In this case, one can define
2
-categories
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{\eta}
(resp.
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{s}
) by modifying the definition of
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{!}
by requiring the sheaves defined only on the generic fiber (resp. special fiber) instead. Moreover, on the level of
2
-morphisms, we require the map
f
in (
2.11
) to be an isomorphism. Then Lemma
2.30
and Lemma
2.31
are true for
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{\eta}
and
\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{s}
. Consider the (strict) symmetric monoidal colax functor
\Psi:\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{\eta}\to\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{s}
defined as follows: On objects, one define
\Psi(A,\mathcal{F})=(A,\Psi\mathcal{F})
. On
1
-morphisms, one define
\Psi(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})=(c,\Psi\mathcal{K},\Psi\mathfrak{c})
. Note that for morphisms

(A_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})\xrightarrow{(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})}(A_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})\xrightarrow{(d,\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{d})}(A_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2}),

there is a
2
-morphism

\begin{split}\Psi((d,\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{d})\circ(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c}))&=(D\times_{A_{1}}C,\Psi(\mathcal{L}\boxtimes_{A_{1,\eta}}\mathcal{K}),\Psi(\mathfrak{d}\circ\mathfrak{c}))\\
&\to(D\times_{A_{1}}C,\Psi(\mathcal{L})\boxtimes_{A_{1,s}}\Psi({\mathcal{K}}),\Psi\mathfrak{d}\circ\Psi\mathfrak{c})\\
&=\Psi(d,\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{d})\circ\Psi(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})\end{split}.

Here, the morphism in the second row is defined by the natural map
\Psi(\mathcal{L})\boxtimes_{A_{1,s}}\Psi({\mathcal{K}})\to\Psi(\mathcal{L}\boxtimes_{A_{1,\eta}}\mathcal{K})
. There is an obvious extension of
\Psi
to
2
-morphisms. This defines the colax functor, which can be made symmetric monoidal by
[
LZ22
, Proposition 3.1]
.
8
8

8

This is for schemes, but the same conclusion holds for algebraic stacks since one can check smooth locally.
For
x=(A,\mathcal{F})\in\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{\eta}
where
\mathcal{F}
is ULA over
S_{\eta}
, note that
\Psi(x)=(A,\Psi\mathcal{F})
is still dualizable since
\Psi\mathcal{F}
is ULA over
S_{s}
(by adapting the proof of
[
LZ22
, Corollary 3.9]
). Consider
F=(c,\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{c})\in\End_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{\eta}}(x)
. Note that we have
2
-commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(x)\otimes\Psi(x^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(x)\otimes\Psi(x^{\vee})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathrm{LZ}(S)_{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(\mathrm{u}_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{u}_{\Psi(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(F)\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(\ev_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev_{\Psi(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

in which the

-morphisms
\gamma
and

are induced from the natural map
\underline{k}_{A_{s}}\to\Psi\underline{k}_{A_{\eta}}
. By the colax property of
\Psi
, we get a
2
-morphism

\eta:(\Fix(C),\Psi(\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)_{\eta}}),\Psi\operatorname{tr}_{S}(\mathfrak{c}))=\Psi(\operatorname{tr}(F,x))\to\operatorname{tr}(\Psi(F),\Psi(x))=(\Fix(C),(\Psi\mathcal{K})|_{\Fix(C)_{s}},\operatorname{tr}_{S}(\Psi\mathfrak{c})).

After checking that this
2
-morphism contains the natural map
\alpha:(\Psi\mathcal{K})|_{\Fix(C)_{s}}\to\Psi(\mathcal{K}|_{\Fix(C)_{\eta}})
, we get
\Psi\operatorname{tr}_{S}(\mathfrak{c})=\operatorname{tr}_{S}(\Psi\mathfrak{c})
.

∎

2.3.
Relative compactification

In this section, we assume that the
G
-variety
X
is affine spherical. We are going to construct a map
\overline{\pi}:\overline{\Bun}_{G}^{X}\to\Bun_{G}
serving as the
relative compactification
of
\pi:\Bun_{G}^{X}\to\Bun_{G}
.

2.3.1.
Preliminaries on spherical varieties

We briefly review the theory of spherical varieties relevant to us. We refer to
[
SW22
]
for a more detailed treatment.
9
9

9

The author is grateful to Yiannis Sakellaridis for explaining the theory of affine degeneration of spherical varieties, especially, Lemma
2.35
. Any mistake in the section is due to the author.

In this section, we work with varieties defined over a field
F
. By a spherical variety, we mean a normal
G
-variety with an open
B
-orbit. For an affine algebraic variety
X
with an action by an algebraic group
G
, we denote
X\sslash G:=\Spec(\mathcal{O}(X)^{G})
. For an affine spherical
G
-variety
X
, the quotient
X\sslash N
is a
T
-variety, on which the
T
-action factors through a quotient
T\twoheadrightarrow T_{X}
, making
X\sslash N
a toric
T_{X}
-variety.

For simplicity, we first discuss the theory when
\mathrm{char}F=0
. In this case, we have a decomposition into irreducible representations
F(X)\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X^{*}(T_{X})}V_{\lambda}
. Define
\mathfrak{c}_{X}\subset X^{*}(T_{X})
such that
\mathcal{O}(X)\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathfrak{c}_{X}}V_{\lambda}
.

For each
G
-invariant discrete valuation
v
on
X
, by restricting
v
to all
B
-eigen functions on
X
, one obtains an element in
X_{*}(T_{X})_{\mathbb{Q}}
. All elements in
X_{*}(T_{X})_{\mathbb{Q}}
obtained in this way generate a cone
\mathcal{V}\subset X_{*}(T_{X})_{\mathbb{Q}}
, which is a fundamental domain for the little Weyl group
W_{X}
acting on
X_{*}(T_{X})_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

There exists a canonical filtration
\{F_{\lambda}\subset\mathcal{O}(X)\}
defined as follows: The subspace
F_{\lambda}\subset\mathcal{O}(X)
is the direct sum of all sub irreducible
G
-representations of
\mathcal{O}(X)
with highest weight
\mu
satisfying
\langle\lambda-\mu,\mathcal{V}\rangle\leq 0
. Then one can form the Rees algebra
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X}):=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X^{*}(T_{X})}F_{\lambda}\otimes e^{\lambda}\subset\mathcal{O}(X\times T_{X})
and define the affine degeneration of
X
to be the variety
\mathscr{X}:=\Spec(\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X}))
. Define
\overline{T_{X,\mathrm{ss}}}:=\Spec(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X^{*}(T_{X}),\langle\lambda,\mathcal{V}\rangle\leq 0}k\cdot e^{\lambda})
, which is a toric
T_{X,\mathrm{ss}}
-variety where
T_{X,\mathrm{ss}}:=T_{X}/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}
. Here
\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}
is the torus satisfying
X_{*}(\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ})\cong\mathcal{V}\cap-\mathcal{V}\cap X_{*}(T_{X})
.

Both varieties
\mathscr{X}
and
\overline{T_{X,\mathrm{ss}}}
carry natural
G\times T_{X}
-actions. There is a natural
G\times T_{X}
-equivariant map
a:\mathscr{X}\to\overline{T_{X,\mathrm{ss}}}
. We use
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}\subset X
to denote the open subvariety whose intersection with each fiber of
a
is the open
G
-orbit of the fiber. Denote
\mathscr{X}^{\circ}:=a^{-1}(T_{X})
. Then there is a canonical isomorphism
\mathscr{X}^{\circ}\cong X\times T_{X}/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}
as
G\times T_{X}
-varieties.

We will need the following fact from
[
SW22
, §2.2.2]
:

Lemma 2.35
.

The stack
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
is representable by a proper algebraic variety.

Proof.

This is mentioned in
[
SW22
, §2.2.2]
without proof. We provide a proof for completeness. First note that
T_{X}(\overline{F})
acts freely on
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}(\overline{F})
. Indeed, one only needs to check that
T_{X}(\overline{F})
acts freely on the
\overline{F}
-points of the special fiber of
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}\to\overline{T_{X,\mathrm{ss}}}
, which follows from the fact that this special fiber is homogeneous horospherical with associated torus
T_{X}
. Second, note that
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}
can be covered by
T_{X}
-stable open affine sub-varieties. Indeed, as a spherical
G\times T_{X}
-variety,
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}
can be covered by
B\times T_{X}
-stable open affine sub-varieties, which are automatically
T_{X}
-stable. These two facts imply that the quotient stack
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
is representable by a scheme, which is clearly normal and of finite type.

We claim that
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
is a spherical
G
-variety. In fact, we only need to check that
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
is separated. Indeed, since
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}
admits a unique closed
G\times T_{X}
-orbit, we know that
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
admits an open affine subset
U
intersecting non-trivially with every
G
-orbit. For any two maps
a_{1},a_{2}:\Spec(R)\to\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
inducing the same map on
\Spec(\mathrm{Frac}(R))
for a discrete valuation ring
R
, via translation by
G
, one can assume that
a_{1},a_{2}
has their image lying in
U
. This implies
a_{1}=a_{2}
, hence, the separatedness of
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
.

Note that the valuations of the
G
-stable divisors of
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
generate exactly its valuation cone. We know that the colored cone of
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
contains the valuation cone, hence, it is a proper algebraic variety.
∎

When
\mathrm{char}F>0
, we make the following assumption:

Assumption 2.36
.

The
G
-representation
\mathcal{O}(X)
admits a good filtration in the sense of
[
Jan03
, §II.4.16]
. Moreover, we assume the good filtration can be chosen to be multiplicative. That is, there exists an increasing filtration
\{F_{\lambda}\subset\mathcal{O}(X)\}_{\lambda\in X^{*}(T_{X})}
10
10

10

The partial order on
X^{*}(T_{X})
is the same as for the filtration
\{F_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in X^{*}(T_{X})}
in characteristic zero case. That is,
\lambda\leq\mu
if and only if
\langle\lambda-\mu,\mathcal{V}\rangle\geq 0
.
such that:

•

For each
\lambda\in X^{*}(T_{X})
,

(2.12)

F_{\lambda}/F_{<\lambda}\cong\left\{\begin{aligned} \nabla_{\lambda}&,\lambda\in\mathfrak{c}_{X}\\
0&,\lambda\notin\mathfrak{c}_{X}\end{aligned}\right.

Here,
\nabla_{\lambda}\in\Rep(G)
is the costandard object
11
11

11

In the notation of
[
Jan03
]
, one has
\nabla_{\lambda}=H^{0}(\lambda)
.
with highest weight
\lambda
;

•

The multiplication on
\mathcal{O}(X)
preserves the filtration
\{F_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in X^{*}(T_{X})}
, that is, it induces
F_{\lambda}\otimes F_{\mu}\to F_{\lambda+\mu}
for
\lambda,\mu\in X^{*}(T_{X})
.

Under this assumption, the entire argument in characteristic zero case can be modified to work using the filtration
\{F_{\lambda}\subset\mathcal{O}(X)\}_{\lambda\in X^{*}(T_{X})}
.

Here are some examples that Assumption
2.36
is satisfied:

Example 2.37

(Group case)
.

When
G=H\times H
and
X=H=H\backslash H\times H
for a split reductive group
H
, by
[
Jan03
, §II.4.20]
, we know that Assumption
2.36
is satisfied. Note that the multiplicativity of the filtration is automatic since
X
is wavefront.
12
12

12

A spherical variety
X
is called
wavefront
if
X_{*}(T)\to X_{*}(T_{X})
maps the dominant cone in
X_{*}(T)_{\mathbb{Q}}
onto
-\mathcal{V}\subset X_{*}(T_{X})_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Example 2.38

(Rankin–Selberg case)
.

When
G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
and
X=\GL_{n}=\GL_{n-1}\backslash\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
, by applying
[
Jan03
, §II.4.24]
, one knows that
\GL_{n-1}\subset\GL_{n}
is a good pair (also called a Donkin pair). Therefore, Assumption
2.36
is satisfied. The multiplicativity of the filtration is automatic since
X
is wavefront.

Example 2.39

(Symmetric varieties)
.

When
X
is a symmetric variety and
F
is algebraically closed, by
[
BS24
, Theorem 2]
, one knows that Assumption
2.36
is satisfied when
\mathrm{char}F\neq 2
.

2.3.2.
Relative compactification

In this section, we assume Assumption
2.36
when
\mathrm{char}F>0
. Moreover, we assume that
X=H\backslash G
is
G
-homogeneous. In this case, we have
\mathscr{X}^{\circ}\subset\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}
.

Define

\overline{\Bun}_{G}^{X}:=\Map(C,\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/G\subset\mathscr{X}/G)/T_{X}

\mathcal{B}:=\Map(C,\overline{T_{X,\mathrm{ss}}})/T_{X}.

Note that
\overline{\Bun}_{G}^{X}
contains an open substack

\Bun_{G}^{X}/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}=\Map(C,\mathscr{X}^{\circ}/G)/T_{X}

and
\mathcal{B}
contains an open substack

*/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}=\Map(C,T_{X,\mathrm{ss}})/T_{X}.

We have a Cartesian diagram

(2.13)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}^{X}/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Bun}_{G}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}

Note that there is a natural map
\overline{\pi}:\overline{\Bun}^{X}_{G}\to\Bun_{G}
.

Proposition 2.40
.

The map
\overline{\pi}:\overline{\Bun}^{X}_{G}\to\Bun_{G}
is representable in proper algebraic spaces.

Proof.

Consider
\overline{\Bun}_{G\times T_{X}}^{X}:=\Map(C,\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/G\times T_{X}\subset\mathscr{X}/G\times T_{X})
. We have a Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Bun}_{G}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Bun}_{G\times T_{X}}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*/T_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{T_{X}}}

where the map
*/T_{X}\to\Bun_{T_{X}}
is induced by the trivial
T_{X}
-bundle over
C
. This implies that
\overline{\Bun}_{G}^{X}\to\overline{\Bun}_{G\times T_{X}}^{X}
is a closed immersion. Therefore, we only need to show that each the natural map

\overline{\pi}_{T_{X}}:\overline{\Bun}_{G\times T_{X}}^{X}\to\Bun_{G}

is representable in proper algebraic spaces after restricting to connected components of
\Bun_{T_{X}}
.

We use
\xi\in C
to denote the generic point of the curve
C
. We first show that
\overline{\pi}_{T_{X}}:\overline{\Bun}_{G\times T_{X}}^{X}\to\Bun_{G}
is representable in algebraic spaces. By
[
Sta25
, 0DSL]
, we only need to check that for each
x=\mathcal{F}_{G}\in\Bun_{G}(\overline{F})
, the fiber
\overline{\pi}_{T_{X}}^{-1}(x)
has trivial stabilizers. In fact, for
y=(\mathcal{F}_{G},\mathcal{F}_{T_{X}},s:\mathcal{F}_{G}\times_{C}\mathcal{F}_{T_{X}}\to\mathscr{X})\in\overline{\pi}_{T_{X}}^{-1}(x)(\overline{F})
, the stabilizer of
y
is the subgroup of
T_{X}
stabilizing the image of
s
, hence is contained in the stabilizer of
s(\widetilde{\xi})\in\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}(\xi)
for any
\widetilde{\xi}\in(\mathcal{F}_{G}\times_{C}\mathcal{F}_{T_{X}})(\xi)
projecting to
\xi
. Note that
T_{X}
acts freely on
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}
. It follows that the stabilizer above must be trivial.

Then we show that the map
\overline{\pi}_{T_{X}}
is proper. Since
\overline{\pi}_{T_{X}}
is of finite type (after restricting to a connected component of
\Bun_{T_{X}}
) and quasi-separated, using the valuative criterion for properness
[
Sta25
, 0CLY]
, we only need to show that for any discrete valuation ring
R
with
D=\Spec R
and
D^{\circ}=\Spec\mathrm{Frac}(R)
, any diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Bun}_{G\times T_{X}}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}}

admits a unique (and up to a unique isomorphism, same for below) dashed arrow making the diagram commutative. Equivalently, this is to say that given any
G
-torsor
a_{G}:\mathcal{F}_{G}\to D\times C
and a
G
-equivariant map
a_{D^{\circ}}:\mathcal{F}_{G}|_{D^{\circ}\times C}\to\mathscr{X}/T_{X}
whose restriction
a_{D^{\circ}}|_{D^{\circ}\times\xi}:\mathcal{F}_{G}|_{D^{\circ}\times\xi}\to\mathscr{X}/T_{X}
has its image lying in
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}\subset\mathscr{X}/T_{X}
, there exists a unique map
a:\mathcal{F}_{G}\to\mathscr{X}/T_{X}
extending
a_{D^{\circ}}
and whose restriction
a|_{D\times\xi}:\mathcal{F}_{G}|_{D\times\xi}\to\mathscr{X}/T_{X}
has image lying in
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}\subset\mathscr{X}/T_{X}
. Note that
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
is a proper algebraic variety by Lemma
2.35
, by valuative criterion for properness, we know that there exists a unique map
a_{\xi}:\mathcal{F}_{G}|_{D\times\xi}\to\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}
extending
a_{D^{\circ}}|_{D^{\circ}\times\xi}
. This implies that there exists a unique map
a_{2}:\mathcal{F}_{G}|_{D\times\xi\cup D^{\circ}\times C}\to\mathscr{X}/T_{X}
extending
a_{D^{\circ}}
and whose restriction to
\mathcal{F}_{G}|_{D\times\xi}
has its image lying in
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}/T_{X}\subset\mathscr{X}/T_{X}
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{G}
is normal,
\mathcal{F}_{G}|_{D\times\xi\cup D^{\circ}\times C}\subset\mathcal{F}_{G}
has codimension two complement, and
\mathscr{X}
is affine, we know that there exists a unique desired map
a:\mathcal{F}_{G}\to\mathscr{X}/T_{X}
.

∎

Remark 2.41
.

When
G=H\times H
and
X=H
for a split reductive group
H
of adjoint type, we obtain the well-known fact that the relative compactification
\overline{\Bun}_{H}\to\Bun_{H}\times\Bun_{H}
is proper. This special case is proved in
[
FKM20
, §A.1]
. Our proof simplifies and generalizes the proof in
loc.cit
.

3.
Geometric trace and special cycle classes

In this section, we relate the minuscule homogeneous special cycle classes in §
1.3.1
and the diagonal cycle classes in §
1.3.2
to the geometric Shtuka construction (introduced in §
2.2.7
) of the corresponding cohomological correspondences. The main results in this section are Theorem
3.2
and Theorem
3.3
.

•

In §
3.1
, we prove a general theorem which will be used in the proof of the main results of this section.

•

In §
3.2
and §
3.3
, we formulate and prove the main results in this section.

3.1.
General formalism in affine homogeneous case

Given an affine homogeneous spherical variety
X
. Consider the map of correspondences

(3.1)

\hbox to231.29pt{\vbox to58.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 115.64502pt\lower-27.59283pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

We fix dominant coweight
\lambda_{I}\in X_{*}(T)_{+}^{I}
and kernel sheaf
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G,I})
supported on
\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Assume Assumption
2.36
is satisfied. For any
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}^{X})_{\mathrm{c}}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{G,I}^{X},\mathcal{K}|_{\Hk_{G,I}^{X}}}(\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}},\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{G,I},\mathcal{K}}(\pi_{!}\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}},\pi_{!}\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Sht_{G,I}^{X}/C^{I},\mathcal{K}|_{\Sht_{G,I}^{X}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}^{BM}(\Sht_{G,I}/C^{I},\mathcal{K}_{\Sht_{G,I}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\Hk,I,!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\Sht,C^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{tr}_{\Sht,C^{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\Sht,I,!}}

is commutative.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{K}
is supported on
\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}\subset\Hk_{G,I}
, we can restrict to the substack
\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}^{X}\subset\Hk_{G,I}^{X}
and
\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}\subset\Hk_{G,I}
, hence all the stacks involved will be algebraic stacks. We will use the same notations for maps restricted to these substacks. By Corollary
2.28
, we only need to show that
\pi_{\Hk,I}
is a composition of maps of correspondences admitting compactification and check the goodness in each step.

For the compactification, we can factor the map of correspondence (
3.1
) as

(3.2)

We only need to show that both
\pi_{1,\Hk,I}
and
\pi_{2,\Hk,I}
admit compactification. For
\pi_{1,\Hk,I}
, one can choose any proper toric embedding
\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}\subset W
and consider

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}^{X}\times C^{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}^{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}^{X}\times C^{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Bun_{G}^{X}\times W)/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}\times C^{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}^{X}\times W)/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Bun_{G}^{X}\times W)/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}\times C^{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}^{X}/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}\times C^{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}^{X}/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}^{X}/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}\times C^{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1,\Hk,I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi}_{1}\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi}_{1,\Hk,I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi}_{1}\times\mathrm{id}}

in which
\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}
acts diagonally on each stack in the middle row. Here, each upper vertical map is induced by the inclusion map
*=\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}\subset W/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}
, and each lower vertical map is the projection to the first factor. Note that the entire middle correspondence admits a map to
W/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}
and the boundary locus is stable because it is the fiber over
\partial W/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}\subset W/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}
. This shows that the factorization above satisfies the condition in Definition
2.26
and
\pi_{1,\Hk,I}
admits compactification.

Here the left and right columns both come from the relative compactification
\Bun_{G}^{X}/\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}\xrightarrow{j}\overline{\Bun}_{G}^{X}\xrightarrow{\overline{\pi}}\Bun_{G}
introduced in §
2.3.2
(whose existence relies on Assumption
2.36
). The middle column is defined as follows: recall that
\overline{\Bun}_{G}^{X}
can be identified with (the quotient by
\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}
of) the moduli of
(\mathcal{E}_{G},s)
where
\mathcal{E}_{G}
is a
G
-bundle on
C
and
s:\mathcal{E}_{G}\to\mathscr{X}
is a
G
-equivariant map which generically has its image lying in
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}\subset\mathscr{X}
. We take
\overline{\Hk}_{G,\lambda_{I}}^{X}
to be the closed substack of
\overline{\Bun}_{G}^{X}\times_{\Bun_{G}}\Hk_{G,\lambda_{I}}
defined as (the quotient by
\mathcal{Z}(X)^{\circ}
of) the moduli of tuples
(\mathcal{E}_{G,1},\mathcal{E}_{G,2},c_{I},a,s)
where
c_{I}\in C^{I}
,
a:\mathcal{E}_{G,1}|_{C-\{c_{I}\}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{E}_{G,2}|_{C-\{c_{I}\}}
is an isomorphism of
G
-bundles over
C-\{c_{I}\}
with its pole at
c_{I}
bounded by
\lambda_{I}
,
s:\mathcal{E}_{G,2}\to\mathscr{X}
is a
G
-equivariant map generically has its image lying in
\mathscr{X}^{\bullet}\subset\mathscr{X}
such that the composition
s\circ a:\mathcal{E}_{G,1}|_{C-\{c_{I}\}}\to\mathscr{X}
can be extended to the entire
\mathcal{E}_{G,1}
. From the definition, it is clear that the middle column, and hence the diagram above, is defined.

2.26
follows from Proposition

j_{!}
preserve constructibility.
∎

3.2.
Minuscule homogeneous special cycles classes

In this section, assume we are in the setting of §
1.3.1
.

Consider the diagram (
1.28
) and take
X=H\backslash G
, we have the following result comparing geometric trace with special cycle class:

Theorem 3.2
.

Assuming Assumption
2.36
, we have

\pi_{\Sht,I,!}[\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}]=\operatorname{tr}_{\Sht,C^{I}}(\pi_{\Hk,I,!}[\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}])\in H_{-d_{\lambda_{I}}+2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{BM}(\Sht_{G,I}/C^{I},\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}}).

Proof.

Take
\mathcal{K}=\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}}
,
\mathcal{F}=\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}}
, and

(3.3)

\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}_{\lambda_{H,I}}=[\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}]\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}},\underline{k}}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}},\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}}\langle-2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}\rangle)

in Theorem
3.1
, we are reduced to show

[\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}]=\operatorname{tr}_{\Sht,C^{I}}([\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}])\in H_{2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{BM}(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}).

This follows directly from Theorem
2.17
.
∎

3.3.
Diagonal cycle classes

In this section, assume we are in the setting of §
1.3.2
and take
G=H\times H
and
X=H
.

Theorem 3.3
.

We have

\langle-,-\rangle_{\lambda_{H,I}}=\operatorname{tr}_{\Sht,C^{I}}(\Delta_{\Hk,I,!}[\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}])\in\Hom^{0}((l_{H,I,!}(\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}|_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}}))^{\otimes 2},\underline{k}_{C^{I}}).

Proof.

By Example
2.37
, Assumption
2.36
is satisfied. Therefore, we can apply Theorem
3.1
. Take
\mathcal{K}=\IC_{\lambda_{I}}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G,I})
,
\mathcal{F}=\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}}
, and

(3.4)

\mathfrak{c}=[\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}]\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{H,I},\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{\otimes 2}}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}},\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}})

in Theorem
3.1
, we are reduced to show

[\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I}]=\operatorname{tr}_{\Sht,C^{I}}([\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}])\in H_{0}^{BM}(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I},\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{\otimes 2}|_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}})).

Note that restriction along the open Schubert cell
\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}^{\circ}\subset\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}
gives an isomorphism

H_{0}^{BM}(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}/C^{I},\IC_{V_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{\otimes 2}|_{\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}})\cong H_{2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{BM}(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}^{\circ}/C).

Applying Theorem
2.17
for the open immersion, we are reduced to show

[\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}^{\circ}/C^{I}]=\operatorname{tr}_{\Sht,C^{I}}([\Hk_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}^{\circ}/\Bun_{H}\times C^{I}])\in H_{2d_{\lambda_{H,I}}}^{BM}(\Sht_{H,\lambda_{H,I}}^{\circ}/C^{I})

which again follows from Theorem
2.17
.
∎

4.
Categorical trace and geometric trace

In this section, we interpret the geometric Shtuka construction of a special cohomological correspondence as a categorical trace. The main result in this section is Theorem
4.9
.

•

In §
4.1
, we review some basic facts about the geometric Langlands conjecture.

•

In §
4.2
, we review the interpretation of Shtuka cohomology as a categorical trace.

•

In §
4.3
, we develop some tools to compute the functoriality of categorical trace in our setting.

•

In §
4.4
, we formulate and prove the main result in this section.

4.1.
Recollections on geometric Langlands

Consider the natural embedding
\iota:\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})
, it admits a right adjoint
\iota_{\mathrm{R}}:\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
. Also, consider
\iota_{2}:\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}^{2})\to\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}^{2})
and its right adjoint
\iota_{2,\mathrm{R}}:\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}^{2})\to\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}^{2})
. We sometimes omit the functor
\iota
(resp.
\iota_{2}
) and regard
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
(resp.
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}^{2})
) as a subcategory of
\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}^{2})
(resp.
\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}^{2})
).

One has the following fundamental result:

Theorem 4.1

(
[
GR25
]
)
.

The functor
\iota_{\mathrm{R}}:\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
coincides with the Beilinson’s spectral projector
\mathsf{P}
defined in
[
AGK+22c
, §13.4.4]
. In particular,
\iota_{\mathrm{R}}
is continuous.

Consider
\Delta_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}^{2})
where
\Delta:\Bun_{G}\to\Bun_{G}^{2}
is the diagonal map and define

\mathrm{u}:=\iota_{R}(\Delta_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}})\in\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}^{2}).

Define

\ev:=\Gamma_{c}\circ\Delta^{*}\circ\iota_{2}:\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}^{2})\to\mathrm{Vect}.

By
[
AGK+22c
, Theorem 16.3.3]
, the exterior tensor product functor

\boxtimes:\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})^{\otimes 2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}^{2})

is an equivalence of categories. In the following, we usually omit the functor
\boxtimes
and do not distinguish detween
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})^{\otimes 2}
and
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}^{2})
. Also, by
[
AGK+22c
, Theorem F.9.7]
, for any finite set
I
, the exterior product

\boxtimes:\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\otimes\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})\to\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})

is an equivalence of categories. Here we abbreviate
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}):=\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}\times\{0\}}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})
. From now on, we do not distinguish between
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\otimes\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})
and
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})
and often omit the exterior product.

Recall the following result from
[
AGK+22b
, Theorem 3.2.2]
:

Theorem 4.2
.

The category
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
admits a self-duality with unit

\mathrm{u}\in\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})^{\otimes 2}

and counit

\ev:\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})^{\otimes 2}\to\mathrm{Vect}.

One of the adjunction map is given by

\alpha:(\mathrm{id}\otimes\ev)(\mathrm{u}\otimes-)=\pr_{1,!}\circ(\mathrm{id}\times\Delta)^{*}(\iota_{2}\circ\iota_{2,\mathrm{R}}(\Delta_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}})\boxtimes-)\to\pr_{1,!}\circ(\mathrm{id}\times\Delta)^{*}((\Delta_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}})\boxtimes-)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{id}

where the first identity follows from definition, the second natural transformation comes from the adjunction map
\iota_{2}\circ\iota_{2,\mathrm{R}}\to\mathrm{id}
, the third isomorphism is given by base change isomorphisms and projection formulas. Another adjunction map
\beta:(\ev\otimes\mathrm{id})(-\otimes\mathrm{u})\to\mathrm{id}
admits a similar description.

Recall the following properties of Hecke operators introduced in §
1.5.3
:

Lemma 4.3
.

(1)

The right adjoint of
T_{V^{I}}:\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})\to\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})
is

T_{c^{*}(V^{I})}\cong\overleftarrow{h}_{*}(\overrightarrow{h}^{!}(-)\otimes^{!}\IC_{V^{I}})\cong\overleftarrow{h}_{!}(\overrightarrow{h}^{*}(-)\otimes\IC_{V^{I}}):\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})\to\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}).

Here
c^{I}\in\End(\check{G}^{I})
is the Cartan involution.

(2)

There is a canonical isomorphism of functors
l_{I,!}(-\otimes T_{V^{I}}(-))\cong l_{I,!}(T_{c^{*}(V^{I})}(-)\otimes-)
.

(3)

The functor
T_{V^{I}}
preserves the full-subcategory
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})\subset\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})
, hence, gives a functor
T_{V^{I}}:\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})\to\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})
.

Proof.

The first is immediate from the usual six-functor formalism (see
[
AGK+22b
, 1.1.5]
). The second follows directly from projection formulas (see
[
AGK+22b
, Lemma 3.4.8]
). The third is
[
AGK+22c
, Theorem 14.2.4]
.
∎

4.2.
Shtuka cohomology as a categorical trace

These is a natural map

(4.1)

\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}((\Frob\times\mathrm{id})_{!}\circ T_{V^{I}},\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{LT}^{\mathrm{Serre}}}l_{I,!}(\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}})

defined as

\begin{split}&\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{QLisse}(C^{I})}((\Frob\times\mathrm{id})_{!}\circ T_{V^{I}},\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}))\\
\cong&(\ev\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ(\Frob_{\Bun_{G}}\times\mathrm{id})_{!}\circ T_{V^{I}\boxtimes\mathrm{triv}}(\mathrm{u}\otimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})\\
=&l_{I,!}\circ(\Frob_{\Bun_{G}}\times\mathrm{id})_{!}\circ T_{V^{I}\boxtimes\mathrm{triv}}(\iota\circ\iota_{\mathrm{R}}(\Delta_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}})\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})\\
\to&l_{I,!}\circ(\Frob_{\Bun_{G}}\times\mathrm{id})_{!}\circ T_{V^{I}\boxtimes\mathrm{triv}}((\Delta_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}})\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})\\
\cong&l_{I,!}(\IC_{V^{I}}|_{\Sht_{G,I}})\end{split}

in which the third map uses the adjunction
\iota\circ\iota_{\mathrm{R}}\to\mathrm{id}
.

By
[
AGK+22a
, Theorem 4.1.2, Theorem 5.5.6]
, we have

Theorem 4.4
.

The map (
4.1
) is an isomorphism.

4.3.
Duality of functors

We would like to apply functoriality of trace construction in §
2.1
to functors

\int_{\mathcal{P},\mathrm{Nilp}}:=\Gamma_{c}(\mathcal{P}\otimes-):\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Vect}

for some
\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})_{\mathrm{c}}
. For this purpose, we need to study its right adjoint
\int_{\mathcal{P},\mathrm{Nilp},\mathrm{R}}
and dual functor
\int_{\mathcal{P},\mathrm{Nilp},\mathrm{R}}^{\vee}
.

We first study the version without nilpotent singular support. For each constructible sheaf

\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})_{\mathrm{c}},

consider the functor

\int_{\mathcal{P}}:=\Gamma_{c}(\mathcal{P}\otimes-):\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Vect}.

Lemma 4.5
.

The functor
\int_{\mathcal{P}}
preserves compact objects, hence, it admits a continuous right adjoint.

Proof.

By
[
AGK+22c
, Proposition F.4.7]
the category
\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})
is generated by compact objects of the form
a_{!}\mathcal{F}_{S}
where
S
is an affine scheme equipped with a map
a:S\to\Bun_{G}
of finite type, and
\mathcal{F}_{S}\in\mathrm{Shv}(S)_{\mathrm{c}}
is a constructible sheaf on
S
. Since
\int_{\mathcal{P}}a_{!}\mathcal{F}_{S}\cong\Gamma_{c}(\mathcal{F}_{S}\otimes a^{*}\mathcal{P})
is a perfect complex, we know
\int_{\mathcal{P}}
preserves compact objects.
∎

The right adjoint of
\int_{\mathcal{P}}
admits the following explicit description: Consider

\int_{\mathcal{P},\mathrm{R}}:=-\otimes\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P}):\mathrm{Vect}\to\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}),

we have an adjoint pair
(\int_{\mathcal{P}},\int_{\mathcal{P},\mathrm{R}})
. The first adjunction map is given by

(4.2)

\alpha_{\mathcal{P}}:\int_{\mathcal{P}}\circ\int_{\mathcal{P},\mathrm{R}}=\Gamma_{c}(-\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P}))\to\Gamma_{c}(-\otimes\omega_{\Bun_{G}})\to\mathrm{id}.

Here, the second map is given by the natural map
\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P})\to\omega_{\Bun_{G}}
, the third arrow uses the adjunction map for the adjoint pair
(f_{!},f^{!})
for
f:\Bun_{G}\to*
. The second adjunction map is given by

(4.3)

\beta_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathrm{id}\to-\otimes(\mathcal{P}\otimes^{!}\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P}))\to(-\otimes\mathcal{P})\otimes^{!}\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P})\to\Gamma_{c}(-\otimes\mathcal{P})\otimes\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P})=\int_{\mathcal{P},\mathrm{R}}\circ\int_{\mathcal{P}}.

Here, the first map uses the adjunction
\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}}\to\mathcal{P}\otimes^{!}\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P})
, the second map comes from the base change map
\Delta^{*}(\mathrm{id}\times\Delta)^{!}\to\Delta^{!}(\Delta\times\mathrm{id})^{*}
for the Cartesian diagram

the third map uses the adjunction map for the adjoint pair
(f_{!},f^{!})
.

By restricting to
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\subset\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})
, one gets functor

\int_{\mathcal{P},\mathrm{Nilp}}=\Gamma_{c}(-\otimes\mathcal{P}):\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Vect}

which has continuous right adjoint

\int_{\mathcal{P},\mathrm{Nilp},\mathrm{R}}=-\otimes\iota_{\mathrm{R}}(\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P})).

The left and right lax-commutative squares in (
2.1
) for the functor
\int_{\mathcal{P},\mathrm{Nilp}}
becomes

(4.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vect}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})^{\otimes 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vect}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Vect}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Here the first natural transformation
\gamma_{\mathcal{P}}
is defined by

\gamma_{\mathcal{P}}:\int_{\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P})}\Delta_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Gamma_{c}(\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P}))\to\Gamma_{c}(\omega_{\Bun_{G}})\to k

and the second natural transformation
\delta_{\mathcal{P}}
is defined by

\begin{split}\delta_{\mathcal{P}}:\Gamma_{c}\circ\Delta^{*}(-)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Gamma_{c}(-\otimes\Delta_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{G}})\\
&\to\Gamma_{c}(-\otimes\Delta_{!}(\mathcal{P}\otimes^{!}\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P})))\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Gamma_{c}(-\otimes\Delta_{!}\Delta^{!}(\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P})))\\
&\to\Gamma_{c}(-\otimes(\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P})))\\
&=\int_{\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{P})}\end{split}.

We want to relate diagrams (
4.4
)(
4.5
) to (
4.6
)(
4.7
).

.

Proof.

4.4.
Main result

4.4.1.
Special cycle classes as geometric trace

Given
\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})_{\mathrm{c}}
and a cohomological correspondence

\mathfrak{c}\in\Hom^{0}(\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}},T_{V^{I}}(\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}}))=\Hom^{0}(T_{c^{*}(V^{I})}(\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}}),\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}})=\mathrm{Corr}_{\Hk_{G,I},\IC_{V^{I}}}(\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}},\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}}),

we can apply the second construction of geometric trace via the diagram (
2.4
), in which we take
S=C^{I}
, the correspondence
(A\xleftarrow{c_{1}}C\xrightarrow{c_{0}}A)=(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I}\xleftarrow{\overleftarrow{h}_{I}}\Hk_{G,I}\xrightarrow{\overrightarrow{h}_{I}}\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})
,
\mathcal{K}=\IC_{V^{I}}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Hk_{G,I})
,
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{P}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C^{I}}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times C^{I})
, and the cohomological correspondence
\mathfrak{c}^{(1)}
.
